We haven’t seen the last of the major tech outages
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
22:48:02 UTC
07:48:02 KST

Over the last week or so, we have seen a nationwide FAA ground stop, a multi-day Outlook outage that brought entire companies to a standstill, and a banking outage at JPMorgan Chase. But the consensus is that there are more to come. Director of National Intelligence Jay Clayton told CNN:
We haven’t seen the last of the major tech outages
Microsoft and the US government agree there are more outages to come.
“The American people should be prepared for a great deal of investment in upgrading our systems; they should be prepared for investment in cybersecurity, and they should be prepared that any time there is a step change in technology, a step change in both compute and applications, there will be bumps in the road.”
Microsoft President Brad Smith issued a similar warning about further outages to CBS, but sounded a much more ominous tone.
“I think we have to be prepared, frankly, for the coming months because this isn’t the type of thing that just gets done in a couple of weeks … The whole information infrastructure of the world is in effect being upgraded right now.”
While the outages over the last week were believed to be caused by cyberattacks, Smith said that AI has made it easier to find flaws, “and then you can either use that knowledge to patch the code, or if you’re a bad actor, you can use that knowledge to try to hack into systems,” he said.
The last few months have also seen an increase in attacks on government and commercial systems, including those at the USDA, JP Morgan, and supermarkets.
Whether outages are brought on by AI-assisted hackers or human error during the rush to patch an aging system, one thing seems certain: there will be more in the coming months.

Sellers are Closing Etsy Stores in Response to Its New AI Training Policy
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
22:24:00 UTC
07:24:00 KST

Sellers are Closing Etsy Stores in Response to Its New AI Training Policy (pcgamer.com) 3
			
		 	
				"I burned my Etsy shop to the ground this week," illustrator Aaron Millard posted on Instagram:
 8 years, 11,200 sales, 3k+ reviews, hundreds in a customer base. Deleted.
Etsy decided that selling on their platform now means letting them train AI on our artwork AND resell that data to third parties, with no way to opt out (in Canada, anyways)...
 
Etsy's new Terms of Use — going into effect December 15 — include this language in their section about "Your Content":
You also recognize our legitimate interest in using it... This also includes the right for us and our third party partners and vendors to use Your Content to develop, train, test and improve artificial intelligence and machine learning technologies, including to improve the Services and/or help keep them safe, subject to any applicable opt-out rights described in our Privacy Policy.  
"My work took years to learn and create," illustrator Millard wrote on Instagram. "I won't let it be stolen. It's not free fuel for an AI slop business model."
Following Etsy's announcement "artists are shutting down their shops in droves," writes Cartoon Brew, "and posting illustrated goodbye notes to tell customers where to find them next."
Ironically, just weeks ago, Etsy announced tighter rules on misleading AI product photos and bot-generated listings. The marketplace still allows sellers to list work generated from their own prompts, as long as they disclose the use of AI to buyers.
Etsy says it uses sellers' content to spot fraud and help shoppers find products, and that it doesn't train models designed to copy artists' work. That said, the vague terms leave open just how its partners and vendors might use that same content...
The artists posting their goodbyes have decided it's worth walking away from that revenue source to keep their work from being used to train AI.
 
"In the wake of that reaction, Etsy vice president of customer operations James Ossman posted a separate update," writes PC Gamer, "saying it's not really like that at all... [T]hat 'we do not use your content to train AI models designed to copy your original work or replace your creativity.'"
"... we're enabling actions like translating your listing descriptions so buyers around the world can discover your items, or featuring your listing photos in Etsy marketing campaigns to showcase your creative work," Ossman wrote. "We also use your content to develop tools that make running your shop easier, like listing optimization tools or stats summaries that help you better understand your shop's performance. As always, your needs and feedback help guide how we build and improve these tools."
As for providing third-party access to that content, Ossman said that too is for "services and tools that can help you run your business," and promised that "when we partner with third parties, we do so thoughtfully to help protect your content and data."
That hasn't satisfied everyone. Ossman's commitment doesn't exist within the actual terms of service, which as seen above states only that Etsy and partners can "use Your Content to develop, train, test and improve artificial intelligence and machine learning technologies." Ossman also noted in his update that the terms "prohibit scraping Etsy or unauthorized use of content from Etsy to train AI models," but that (as far as I can tell) applies to Etsy users and unauthorized third-parties, not Etsy itself or those it licenses...
Others also complain that AI training should be opt-in rather than on by default — and worse, there's apparently no opt-out option available at all, so users who are opposed to having their work used for AI training for any reason are out of luck.
Ironically, the blowup over Etsy's embrace of AI training comes just weeks after it earned plaudits for banning AI-generated recipes and patterns, a move Ossman said was necessary to establish "trust" in the platform.

‘You Can See Everything’ has made more than $100M worldwide
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
21:42:48 UTC
06:42:48 KST

“You Can See Everything,” Nathan Fielder and Lance Oppenheim’s documentary about Theranos founder Elizabeth Holmes, reached an impressive box office milestone this weekend, becoming one of the few documentaries to make more than $100 million worldwide.
Variety reports that almost all of the film’s $102.4 million box office has come from U.S. audiences, from whom it has grossed $93.4 million so far.
“You Can See Everything” will be opening in many more countries over the next few months — starting with Mexico, Brazil and Argentina on Nov. 25 and 26 — so its box office total could end up significantly higher.
And while the documentary was already an undeniable commercial success for indie studio A24 (especially with a reported budget of $7 million), it could now surpass the records set by the highest grossing documentaries of all time — “March of the Penguins” ($127 million globally) and “Fahrenheit 9/11” ($222 million globally).
Next up: Maybe the International Space Station? And the Oscars?

OpenAI’s chief scientist has thoughts on how to limit AI development
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
21:14:07 UTC
06:14:07 KST

A new blog post from OpenAI’s chief scientist Jakub Pachocki discusses potential limits on AI development, with a focus on a measurement called perplexity.
Slowing AI development became a particularly hot topic after a former OpenAI and Anthropic researcher said the companies were “gambling with our lives,” and even Anthropic’s IPO filing suggested that the technology could pose “catastrophic or existential risks to society.”
Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei then published a plan to “pace the frontier,” seemingly endorsed by OpenAI CEO Sam Altman and SpaceX CEO Elon Musk. Among other things, Amodei called for companies to agree to “common safety standards as well as limits on the rate of unchecked AI progress.”
But to establish a speed limit on AI development, you need to agree on how you measure speed. Pachocki’s post — described by Altman as “the most important thing OpenAI has published in a while” — suggests perplexity (not to be confused with the company of the same name) could be that measurement.
In Pachochki’s argument, benchmarks that focus on AI’s “downstream capabilities” have quickly become outdated as the technology advances. But perplexity — which “measures how well a model predicts the next token on average; lower perplexity means better predictions” — has remained “informative.”
Pachochki’s post includes a chart of how the perplexity of OpenAI’s models has fallen since GPT 3.5.
“GPT‑4, at ~0.91, was proficient at utilizing chain-of-thought reasoning productively, which motivated the subsequent targeted elicitation of this capability and the creation of modern reasoning models,” he wrote. “Now, as we have reached losses below 0.8, we have seen the emergence of transformative capabilities in fields like cybersecurity and mathematics.”
So in his view, perplexity could provide the basis for “a shared benchmark for general intelligence,” which AI companies could then use to establish “common limits.” With perplexity serving as a standard measurement for AI progress, the companies would commit not to go past a certain threshold “until our ability to understand and monitor AI behavior achieves a certain concrete milestone.”

GLP-1 drugs could have a surprisingly large environmental payoff
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
21:07:11 UTC
06:07:11 KST

GLP-1 weight-loss medicines such as Ozempic and Wegovy could affect much more than the waistlines of the people who take them. If widely used, our new research suggests these appetite-suppressing medicines could transform the global food system and free up vast areas of agricultural land.
The global food system is ultimately driven by demand. If people eat less, producers respond, affecting production, prices, trade and how much land is needed. We used a powerful computer simulation to explore scenarios ranging from GLP-1 use concentrated in high-income countries to widespread global access.
With widespread use of these medicines among adults living with obesity, we estimate that reduced calorie intake could prevent up to 6.6 million deaths each year by 2050, while freeing up as much as 32 million hectares of agricultural land – an area slightly larger than Italy – for other uses.
The biggest effects, however, occurred when reduced calorie intake was combined with a shift towards healthier diets. In this scenario, up to 7 million deaths could be avoided each year by 2050, while around 46 million hectares of agricultural land would have become available for other uses. This suggests that alongside weight-loss medicines, we need to make healthier options more widely available, affordable and more appealing.
The food system could respond in unexpected ways
Food systems are interconnected across countries. If demand falls in one part of the world, this can affect farming, trade and food prices elsewhere, with consequences for people who are not taking the medications at all.
Lower demand from GLP-1 users, for instance, could make food cheaper. That sounds positive, but cheaper food could also encourage greater consumption among non-users, offsetting some of the intended benefit. The effects of widespread GLP-1 use, in other words, cannot be understood just by looking at individual patients’ weight loss. They ripple through the global food system.
It’s tempting to read these findings as an argument for putting more people on weight-loss medication. That would miss the bigger picture.
Obesity has often been framed as a “lifestyle disease”, blaming individual people for not having enough willpower. But diets are shaped by what food is available, what it costs, how it’s marketed, where shops are located and what’s most convenient. In many high-income countries, it is remarkably easy to consume large amounts of cheap, energy-dense food. Society tells people to make healthier choices while surrounding them with an environment that often encourages the opposite.
The UK government commissioned national food strategy, finalised in 2021, made this point clearly: improving diets can’t simply be about asking individuals to choose better. The wider food environment needs to change to make the better choice the easy one.
Our findings shouldn’t be taken to mean that people living with obesity have a responsibility to lose weight to reduce the environmental impacts of food production. Rather, our research examines the wider consequences that changes in food demand could have across the food system.
Changing the food system alongside medicine
A healthier diet is not necessarily just a diet with fewer calories. It could mean more fruit, vegetables and other nutritious foods, alongside less excessive consumption of foods linked to poor health. These changes affect not only human health but also agriculture, land use and the environment.
Rather than asking whether GLP-1 medications or healthier diets are the answer, let’s ask how they can work together. Imagine a food system where healthier foods are affordable and accessible, unhealthy options aren’t relentlessly promoted, agricultural policy supports nutritious and sustainable production, and people who need medical treatment can access it. That combination could be far more powerful than any single intervention.
GLP-1 medicines can play an important role in tackling obesity, but they shouldn’t become a technological fix that allows the wider food system to escape scrutiny. They can help people eat less in an unhealthy food environment, but the long-term goal should be to make it easier and more affordable to have a healthier, more sustainable diet.
The remarkable thing about GLP-1 medications may not be that they change how much food people eat. It may be that they show us, with new clarity, just how closely human health, food production and environmental health are connected, and how much could be gained if we tackled all three together.

A Beginner's Guide to Local AI with Inkling, Qwen, and Unsloth
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
21:01:16 UTC
06:01:16 KST

A beginner's guide to Local AI with Inkling, Qwen, and Unsloth
We have AI at home! Set up your own free personal ChatGPT on your PC with Unsloth and Inkling or Qwen. It's private, and the whole house can use it.
By Jerry James ·
With the launch of open-weight models like Qwen, cloud-based LLM providers seem like they're more of infrastructure or appliance businesses when it comes to most routine tasks that an AI model can handle.
OpenAI and Anthropic have displayed a pattern of dropping model quality in the 'latest' version and controlling exactly what you're allowed to run on their models. Add to this ballooning costs and privacy concerns, and we get a pretty good case for moving away from this 'convenient' cloud experience.
That's basically where I was too. I wanted to offload certain token-heavy research, data collection, or coding tasks to an AI model without staring at usage limits all day. So, as any reasonable human being would do, I decided to set up my own local AI: a Claude and ChatGPT replacement that runs at home on my own hardware.
Since this was my first attempt, my primary focus was keeping it simple and hosting it on Windows. If you're a beginner, you can follow along to get one going on your home network too.
Once you have the hardware sorted, it takes about 10-15 minutes (excluding download times).
The short version
Install Unsloth Desktop, download a quantized AI model that fits your GPU's VRAM, set your context size, and turn on LAN access so everyone at home can use it.
What Do You Need to Run a Local AI at Home?
Not much (other than the hardware). Here's a short list:
- A Windows PC with a graphics card (GPU). The more video memory (VRAM) it has, the bigger the model it can run. I'm using an RTX 5090 with 32GB VRAM for this setup.
- Unsloth Desktop. A free app that 'serves' the AI model to you through a chat interface. Get it from Unsloth.
- An AI model sized to fit your GPU. We'll pick one in the next section.
That's it. No terminal commands, Python environments, or subscriptions required.
Which AI Model Can Your GPU Run?
It depends on how much VRAM your graphics card has. As a rough guide, 8-12GB of VRAM handles smaller 9B-12B models, while 32GB comfortably runs 27B-35B models with plenty of room for long conversations.
On consumer-grade hardware, we'll need smaller models. The full-size Qwen model sits at around 56GB and won't really fit on a consumer graphics card unless you're running a Mac with unified memory. That's where quantization comes in.
What Is Quantization?
Quantization shrinks a model by compressing weights and moving to a lower precision. You basically trade some accuracy for a model that actually fits on your GPU and runs faster.
The same photo stored at 8, 4 and 2 bits per pixel. A model's weights lose detail the same way going from Q8 to Q4 to Q2.
If you're wondering whether this tradeoff is worth it, well, it depends. You'll find that certain types of workloads don't really care and give similar outputs, while others may be nearly unusable.
Which Model Should You Run for Your VRAM?
For this guide, we'll be going with Qwen3.8-27B with 4-bit quantization on my system with an RTX 5090. Going up a tier is an option thanks to its 32GB of VRAM, but I wanted to allow for a larger context window (how much of the conversation the model can keep in mind at once) rather than cramming in the largest model that fits.
Qwen3.8-27B at each precision with a 128K context, from our VRAM calculator. At 4-bit, the model and the context fit a 32 GB card with room to spare.
If you want to run a model of this caliber with excellent performance, a hilariously overpriced RTX 5090 (remember when $1,999 felt too high?) is unfortunately still not a terrible 'deal' these days. The other option is way more expensive enterprise-grade GPUs.
If you're on a GPU with less VRAM, you can still get something very usable going right away. The table below gives you a rough idea of where you can start.
Small
- 8-12 GB
- UD-Q4_K_S or Q4_K_M
- 8K-16K
Medium
- 16 GB
- Qwen3-14B, gpt-oss-20b (MoE), Gemma 4 26B (MoE), Qwen3-Coder-30B (MoE), Qwen3.6-35B (MoE), Inkling 2 Mini (MoE)
- Q4_K_M or Q5_K_M
- 16K-32K
Large
- 24 GB
- UD-Q4_K_M or Q4_K_S
- 16K-32K
Extra Large
- 32 GB+
- UD-Q4_K_XL or Q6_K
- 64K-128K+
Models marked MoE (mixture of experts) only run a few billion of their parameters for each token. They keep a decent speed on a smaller card with the rest held in system RAM, which is why a 35B model sits in the 16 GB row. On an RTX 5060 Ti 16GB, our calculator puts Qwen3.6-35B at about 47 tokens per second this way.
Feel free to experiment with several models until you find one that fits your needs. You can also use GPUPartner's VRAM Calculator to get an estimate.
Can You Run Inkling 2 on an RTX 5090?
Inkling 2 Mini, yes. Inkling 2 Small, no.
Inkling 2 Mini is the newest model in this size class. Thinking Machines Lab released it on November 17, 2026: 26.4B parameters, Apache 2.0, vision support and a 1M-token context. Unsloth's recommended file, UD-Q4_K_XL, is 17.6 GB. That is within 0.1 GB of the Qwen3.8-27B file in this guide, so it sits on a 32GB card the same way and leaves about 14GB for context.
Nothing else in this guide changes. Update Unsloth Desktop first, because Inkling 2 needs a recent build, then search for Inkling 2 Mini in the same drop-down in Step 2.
2-bit
- 10-11 GB
- 12 GB card
3-bit
- 13-15 GB
- 16 GB card
4-bit (recommended)
- 17-18 GB
- 24 GB card
8-bit (lossless)
- 24 GB
- 32 GB card
Those figures are Unsloth's. They count RAM and VRAM together and cover the model alone, so a long context needs more. Inkling 2 Mini is a mixture-of-experts model with 5.4B parameters active, so the 4-bit file also runs on a 16 GB card with the remainder in system RAM, a little slower.
Inkling 2 Small is small in name only. It is a 594B model, and the smallest file Unsloth publishes is 171.7 GB. That is unified-memory territory: a 256GB Mac Studio or a DGX Station, not a graphics card.
Can You Run a Local AI on a Mac?
Yes. You'll find good alternatives in Apple's Mac lineup thanks to its unified memory. Macs are pretty good at running larger models, even if they don't offer the best performance. Just make sure you grab the MLX flavor that is custom-built for Apple Silicon.
The same trade applies to unified-memory PCs like the MSI PRO MAX EDGE AI+ 11M and NVIDIA's DGX Spark.
How Do You Set Up a Local AI with Unsloth Desktop?
It takes three steps: install Unsloth Desktop, download your model, and set the context size. I'll go over these steps in more detail below so you can follow along.
Step 1: Install Unsloth Desktop
First, we'll need a solution that 'serves' the AI model to you. My pursuit of simplicity narrowed my choices down to LM Studio or Unsloth Desktop. Both abstract away a ton of terminal commands and handle Python environments and dependencies behind a nice, simple UI, which makes them excellent picks for a first home setup.
I landed on Unsloth Desktop mostly because, well, I liked the name better. Also, it didn't try to push the very thing I was trying to escape when I opened its website: a subscription.
The community seems to like it overall too, so that's another point in its favor.
Once you've clicked through the installer and launched Unsloth, you'll see a window like this one.
Unsloth Desktop on first launch, before any model is loaded.
Step 2: Download a Quantized Model
On the top left of this main window, you can pick the model that you want to use from the drop down. We'll be using Qwen3.8-27B.
The model picker. Qwen3.8-27B is first on the Recommended list.
You should see a submenu like the one below when you click on a model. I'll be using Unsloth's UD-Q4_K_XL version. The M and S versions are smaller, but the XL version behaves much closer to the full-size model without filling up our VRAM, so it seemed like a good pick for my GPU.
Every quantization of Qwen3.8-27B with its download size. The gear next to the downloaded one holds the context setting.
Step 3: Set the Context Size
Once the download completes, you should see a gear icon to the right of the model as shown in the screenshot above. Here, you can set your context size manually or leave it on Auto if you aren't sure how much you need.
I'll be setting mine to 128K (unquantized) and might reassess after I've run the model through a few of my routine tasks for a few weeks. Again, you can use this VRAM calculator to help you with these numbers for your specific GPU.
At this point, our first local AI is up and running, and you can start chatting with it right inside Unsloth.
Remember, your mileage may vary depending on your setup.
How Do You Share a Local AI Across Your Home Network?
Short answer: Unsloth Desktop has LAN sharing built in. Set a Remote password, start LAN access, and share the address it gives you with anyone on your home network.
Once Unsloth Desktop is set up with a model, you might want to give family members or friends access too. For now, we'll only serve it over our local network, so my family can use it without needing physical access to this PC.
Head into Unsloth Desktop settings (gear icon on bottom left), and look for the 'Remote & LAN' tab.
Settings, Remote & LAN. LAN access is the second card. The Remote access card above it is for a later part.
Most of this section will be greyed out until you set a Remote password. Once you've set a password, you'll see a 'Start' button for LAN access. Click that and you should see an address that anyone on your home network can open in a browser to use a local AI model within a chat interface. They'll sign in as 'unsloth' with the Remote password you set.
Just keep in mind that the PC needs to stay on with Unsloth running for anyone to reach it.
Can You Access Your Local AI Across the Internet? Soon™
Not with this setup alone. Everything so far is limited to your local home network.
If you just need a chat interface like ChatGPT, Gemini, etc. to work within your home network, you're basically done at this point! You and anyone on your network can start talking to an AI model hosted on your machine right away.
In the next parts, we'll cover performance, tunneling, as well as setting up an agent that runs locally and uses only the web tools you allow it to use. This will let you automate more complex tasks like coding, research, and so much more without being tied to a subscription or usage limits and censorship.
As always, my focus remains simplicity that allows my family to use it without too much guidance or help.
PS. If you have any recommendations for agentic harnesses or even models to try, feel free to let me know and I'll give them a go!
Sources
- Free, open-source app for macOS, Windows and Linux; runs GGUF and MLX models
- Remote & LAN settings, LAN access start and port behaviour
- Official 16-bit weights (~56 GB) and parameter count
- Quantized files and sizes, including UD-Q4_K_XL (~18 GB)
- 26.4B parameters, Apache 2.0, 1M context, UD-Q4_K_XL at 17.6 GB, memory by precision
- 594B parameters, smallest file 171.7 GB
- Official weights, released November 17, 2026
- 32 GB GDDR7 consumer GPU used for this build

Models marked MoE (mixture of experts) only run a few billion of their parameters for each token. They keep a decent speed on a smaller card with the rest held in system RAM, which is why a 35B model sits in the 16 GB row. On an RTX 5060 Ti 16GB, our calculator puts Qwen3.6-35B at about 47 tokens per second this way.
Feel free to experiment with several models until you find one that fits your needs. You can also use GPUPartner's VRAM Calculator to get an estimate.
Can You Run Inkling 2 on an RTX 5090?
Inkling 2 Mini, yes. Inkling 2 Small, no.
Inkling 2 Mini is the newest model in this size class. Thinking Machines Lab released it on November 17, 2026: 26.4B parameters, Apache 2.0, vision support and a 1M-token context. Unsloth's recommended file, UD-Q4_K_XL, is 17.6 GB. That is within 0.1 GB of the Qwen3.8-27B file in this guide, so it sits on a 32GB card the same way and leaves about 14GB for context.
Nothing else in this guide changes. Update Unsloth Desktop first, because Inkling 2 needs a recent build, then search for Inkling 2 Mini in the same drop-down in Step 2.
2-bit
- 10-11 GB
- 12 GB card
3-bit
- 13-15 GB
- 16 GB card
4-bit (recommended)
- 17-18 GB
- 24 GB card
8-bit (lossless)
- 24 GB
- 32 GB card
Those figures are Unsloth's. They count RAM and VRAM together and cover the model alone, so a long context needs more. Inkling 2 Mini is a mixture-of-experts model with 5.4B parameters active, so the 4-bit file also runs on a 16 GB card with the remainder in system RAM, a little slower.
Inkling 2 Small is small in name only. It is a 594B model, and the smallest file Unsloth publishes is 171.7 GB. That is unified-memory territory: a 256GB Mac Studio or a DGX Station, not a graphics card.
Can You Run a Local AI on a Mac?
Yes. You'll find good alternatives in Apple's Mac lineup thanks to its unified memory. Macs are pretty good at running larger models, even if they don't offer the best performance. Just make sure you grab the MLX flavor that is custom-built for Apple Silicon.
The same trade applies to unified-memory PCs like the MSI PRO MAX EDGE AI+ 11M and NVIDIA's DGX Spark.
How Do You Set Up a Local AI with Unsloth Desktop?
It takes three steps: install Unsloth Desktop, download your model, and set the context size. I'll go over these steps in more detail below so you can follow along.
Step 1: Install Unsloth Desktop
First, we'll need a solution that 'serves' the AI model to you. My pursuit of simplicity narrowed my choices down to LM Studio or Unsloth Desktop. Both abstract away a ton of terminal commands and handle Python environments and dependencies behind a nice, simple UI, which makes them excellent picks for a first home setup.
I landed on Unsloth Desktop mostly because, well, I liked the name better. Also, it didn't try to push the very thing I was trying to escape when I opened its website: a subscription.
The community seems to like it overall too, so that's another point in its favor.
Once you've clicked through the installer and launched Unsloth, you'll see a window like this one.
Unsloth Desktop on first launch, before any model is loaded.
Step 2: Download a Quantized Model
On the top left of this main window, you can pick the model that you want to use from the drop down. We'll be using Qwen3.8-27B.
The model picker. Qwen3.8-27B is first on the Recommended list.
You should see a submenu like the one below when you click on a model. I'll be using Unsloth's UD-Q4_K_XL version. The M and S versions are smaller, but the XL version behaves much closer to the full-size model without filling up our VRAM, so it seemed like a good pick for my GPU.
Every quantization of Qwen3.8-27B with its download size. The gear next to the downloaded one holds the context setting.
Step 3: Set the Context Size
Once the download completes, you should see a gear icon to the right of the model as shown in the screenshot above. Here, you can set your context size manually or leave it on Auto if you aren't sure how much you need.
I'll be setting mine to 128K (unquantized) and might reassess after I've run the model through a few of my routine tasks for a few weeks. Again, you can use this VRAM calculator to help you with these numbers for your specific GPU.
At this point, our first local AI is up and running, and you can start chatting with it right inside Unsloth.
Remember, your mileage may vary depending on your setup.
How Do You Share a Local AI Across Your Home Network?
Short answer: Unsloth Desktop has LAN sharing built in. Set a Remote password, start LAN access, and share the address it gives you with anyone on your home network.
Once Unsloth Desktop is set up with a model, you might want to give family members or friends access too. For now, we'll only serve it over our local network, so my family can use it without needing physical access to this PC.
Head into Unsloth Desktop settings (gear icon on bottom left), and look for the 'Remote & LAN' tab.
Settings, Remote & LAN. LAN access is the second card. The Remote access card above it is for a later part.
Most of this section will be greyed out until you set a Remote password. Once you've set a password, you'll see a 'Start' button for LAN access. Click that and you should see an address that anyone on your home network can open in a browser to use a local AI model within a chat interface. They'll sign in as 'unsloth' with the Remote password you set.
Just keep in mind that the PC needs to stay on with Unsloth running for anyone to reach it.
Can You Access Your Local AI Across the Internet? Soon™
Not with this setup alone. Everything so far is limited to your local home network.
If you just need a chat interface like ChatGPT, Gemini, etc. to work within your home network, you're basically done at this point! You and anyone on your network can start talking to an AI model hosted on your machine right away.
In the next parts, we'll cover performance, tunneling, as well as setting up an agent that runs locally and uses only the web tools you allow it to use. This will let you automate more complex tasks like coding, research, and so much more without being tied to a subscription or usage limits and censorship.
As always, my focus remains simplicity that allows my family to use it without too much guidance or help.
PS. If you have any recommendations for agentic harnesses or even models to try, feel free to let me know and I'll give them a go!

The New 'Riemann Hypothesis' Proof Has Been Confirmed in Mathlib/Lean Communities
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
20:51:00 UTC
05:51:00 KST

The New 'Riemann Hypothesis' Proof Has Been Confirmed in Mathlib/Lean Communities (theguardian.com) 33
			
		 	
				"Experts say the claim checks out," reports the Guardian.
"We have full confidence that Alpöge's proof of the Riemann Hypothesis is correct, and we congratulate him on this achievement," reads Saturday's announcement on the Lean prover community blog:
The codebase depends directly only on Mathlib. Over the past day, the Lean and Mathlib communities have rallied to verify the proof's correctness. We hereby certify that both the statement and the proof of the Riemann Hypothesis are correct...
[Besides checking the code] The exported raw proof has been checked by several proof checkers, including Lean's official kernel, the con-leche and con-ron checkers, and several independent checkers contributed by third parties and implemented in different programming languages. The checkers con-leche and con-ron come with a formal proof, relative to set theory, that they cannot accept a false proof, thus addressing concerns that Lean's logic itself might have flaws that the proof could exploit...   
We look forward to all the activity in the mathematical community that will help explain the new ideas in this proof.  
"The proof evidently marks another breakthrough in the use of AI for complicated math problems," writes the Guardian, "even as mathematicians express uneasiness around extremely lucrative private companies dishing out millions of dollars for the technology to solve problems."
"Claude and I have had a great time together with such breadth in recent months, but this time Claude far outpaced me near the finish line," Alpöge wrote, while anthropomorphizing the AI software. "No, Claude flew, and danced, and sparkled near that finish line."  
Scientific American captured more mixed reactions Friday:
As Massachusetts Institute of Technology mathematician Andrew Sutherland once put it to me: "An AI that can prove the Riemann hypothesis is not one I'd want to meet. If that happens, mathematicians having jobs will be the least of our problems."
"This is indeed an historic moment," Sutherland tells me today after he had independently compiled Alpöge's result in Lean. "The world has changed.... I know there will be a wide range of reactions in our community, but to me this is a human triumph, and a day for celebration." Sutherland says that it's worth celebrating the human work behind the development of Lean and LLMs — and, for that matter, the millennia of mathematical achievements upon which today's advance rests. But processing the earthshaking reality of what has occurred "is going to take some time."
"There is much work to be done to understand the mathematical details of the proof," he says. "And I can't wait to dig into them. But perhaps the single most important step in the proof was building a machine that can think."

I did a meditation and it sort of changed my personality?!
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
20:41:59 UTC
05:41:59 KST

In March 2025, I went on a Jhourney meditation retreat that was one of the most profound experiences of my life, and deeply reshaped how I related to myself.
If you had told my pre-retreat self that this was going to happen, I wouldn’t have believed you. I’d done some meditation before—including a 10-day vipassana retreat—and while it didn’t do nothing, I was underwhelmed. I felt pretty resistant to spending that much time staring at the inside of my head: it seemed really hard to actually trust anything I learned that way, because it was such a complicated system. And besides, my brain seemed to work fine the way it was.
I’d read some writing by serious meditators who seemed to be having a different experience, filled with insights and bliss, but it wasn’t one that I felt particularly drawn to. That much navel-gazing and wireheading felt self-indulgent, and anyway they seemed weird enough that whatever they were doing probably wouldn’t work for me.
Then, in a moment of life imitating Bay Area House Party, we got Jhourney, a meditation retreat startup promising to speedrun you to the bliss part (tagline at the time: “Life-changing meditative bliss used to take thousands of hours to learn. We teach it in a week.”) When I heard them strongly recommended not only by the serious meditators I’d been reading, but also by a couple more hard-nosed coworkers, I decided to see what the hype was about.
Jhourney teaches a style of meditation nominally focused on altered states called jhanas. Jhanas are blissful-feeling states that you can “relax into” if you release enough of what you might call tension or clenching or tanha, and then focus on an enjoyable open-hearted sensation like smiling, a joyful memory, or feeling care for a loved one. If done right, you can start a feedback loop of enjoying the enjoyment, and end up in a state of deeply absorbed euphoria.
I say Jhourney is nominally focused on that because the jhanas are actually kind of a Trojan horse for improving your life and relationships, which somehow doesn’t sell as well as wireheading. Jhourney’s unofficial motto is “come for the bliss, stay for the personal growth.” The growth happens because a lot of the tension you’re trying to release in jhana meditation is downstream of repressed or “stuck” feelings, either small ones (“I don’t want to be hearing that sound over there” leading to micro-clenching the head and neck) or big ones (“I can’t let myself feel this sadness or I’ll cry and that would be shameful” leading to tensing the various face and chest muscles that would otherwise be helping you cry). In order to get into jhana states, a lot of people (like me!) end up having to work on untangling knots of emotional clenching, and this usually leads to a lot of growth.
My first experience of my Jhourney retreat was showing up and being handed an 80-page handbook of meditation instructions.
This already blew my mind a little, because I had never read anything about spirituality or emotions that I considered good. The “best” stuff I’d read about the mind was vapid pop-psychology, padding out truisms with engaging fluff until the result somehow became a marketable book. The rest was indistinguishable from astrology—it was never clear enough that I could tell whether it applied to me, which meant it didn’t seem to make falsifiable predictions. As for spiritual writing, it mostly felt like word-association mumbo-jumbo, with authors throwing around words that had the right vibes but that I wasn’t sure they themselves even really understood. Heck, the previous meditation retreat I’d been on had included watching an hour-long recording of the now-dead founder talking about how he could sense quantum vibrations.
The Jhourney handbook didn’t have these problems, because it was written by software engineers. It was epistemically conservative and shockingly lucid, completely mumbo- and jumbo-free. To help with the inherently fuzzy language, they explained everything from multiple angles. They gave clear gears-level explanations of why they thought things worked the way they did. They even took a detour to explain their teaching philosophy so that students were oriented about why they were doing what they were doing. It was masterful pedagogy.
Between the written instructions and the teachings from live meditation sessions, pretty soon I could get to a point that felt like the “on-ramp” to a jhana. But every time I got there, I would get stressed and “grabby” about what to do next, and “fall off” the ramp.
By the time I got to day 4 of the retreat, I was feeling really frustrated about this, in a way that was starting to loop on itself. I was frustrated that I couldn’t reach jhana, and frustrated that the frustration was making things harder. I tried Jhourney’s recommended way of working with negative emotions, which was a self-compassion meditation. But no matter how much I tried, it didn’t really resonate; I couldn’t cultivate any emotional experience of self-compassion.
In fact, I realized I couldn’t remember ever having had a real emotional experience of receiving compassion, and that that was probably kind of fucked up.
A bunch of my friends were on the same retreat. Since I had no access to self-compassion, of course I used this realization to start worrying that they would judge me for having a hard time meditating because I was fucked up, adding even more layers to the feedback loop of doom.
Days 4–6 were pretty bad. Every day Jhourney gave us a survey of “how was your day on a scale of 1-10 with 5 as a normal day?” I rated day 6 a 1.5. (To their credit, Jhourney knew this meant I needed extra help and offered lots of 1:1 time with different facilitators, although none of it made that much difference.)
At this point, in one of my desperate attempts to think myself out of my feelings pickle, I started thinking a lot about the metaphor of the elephant and the rider. The idea is that people usually identify with everything that happens in their mind, but this is actually a confusion; it’s more accurate to think of “you,” the conscious part, as a small human rider perched atop a large lumbering elephant that often does its own thing. The only things that are actually in the rider’s action space are things like “say words to the elephant,” “visualize mental images to the elephant,” “direct attention to a particular aspect of experience,” etc. Everything else—including all emotions and many thoughts—is the elephant’s job.
In this frame, the fact that the self-compassion meditation wasn’t working for me wasn’t really “my” fault. Whether the emotional experience of self-compassion happened was up to the elephant, and the only thing “I” (the rider) could do was to keep gently saying the words or doing the technique or whatever, and wait for the elephant to steer, at its own pace.
Around that point I decided to give up on the Jhourney curriculum and just do whatever seemed like it would feel nice.✻ Ironically, Jhourney more or less recommends doing this in various parts of the handbook, but I was too into following rules to follow the part of the rules where they told me to stop following the rules. I broke the rule of silence to ask for some reassurance from a close friend who was also on the retreat. When I got back to meditating, I made up a technique for myself where I imagined sending love and compassion to the elephant instead of to “myself.” This turned out to be a cheat code: I immediately felt an outpouring of warmth, and the reassuring things I was telling myself started to emotionally resonate.
I really wish I could describe better what happened after that, but unfortunately it was a while ago and involved a lot of subtle mental movements, plus it’s hard to track what’s happening while a chunk of your psyche is liquefying. I remember a sort of sense of “going inside” the feeling of compassion and love for the elephant, having it take up more and more of my attention, and feel more and more deep, rich and meaningful. Then some kind of rush of energy and a full-body frisson that I’ve learned often happens when my brain has rearranged itself.† I don’t think this strictly qualifies as a jhana by Jhourney’s rubric, because it wasn’t stable and self-sustaining, but it otherwise felt close to how they describe first jhana.
When I got up and walked outside, I gradually noticed that the world felt pretty different. I realized that I’d always carried a “refrigerator hum” of subtle body tension that was now gone. Walking around the retreat center looking at the views felt amazing, like I could have spent days just melting into the landscape. The Lipton peppermint tea at the hot drink station tasted unreasonably good. I would sometimes tear up with joy or gratitude for no reason. I felt much deeper care and connection with my friends.
Maybe that makes it sound a little gooey and impersonal, like I took a drug that replaced my normal self with a puddle of love or something. The actual experience was the opposite; somehow it felt like this was all happening because it was easier to be myself. The care and connection wasn’t coming from some foreign love puddle; it came out because a bunch of psychic gunk had sloughed off and made it easier for the real essence of Ben-ness to shine through.
And then I used my newfound mastery of emotions and self-compassion to live happily ever after flailed around trying to integrate everything back into normal life.
One of the big changes I noticed after the retreat was that my emotions got 2–3× more intense. I think this was net positive in the end, but it made coming back to work hard—I had a stressful role managing a large team, and I also needed to catch up from a week of being incommunicado, which was extra stressful. And it wasn’t exactly helpful that suddenly, if someone was annoying on Slack, it would sometimes make me want to shoot flames out my ears.
Over the next couple weeks, the intensity settled back down and the changes in my moment-to-moment experience went maybe 80% of the way back to normal (e.g., I stopped spontaneously tearing up in normal circumstances). Some of them stayed, though:
- A lot of my shame/cringe reflex turned into a self-compassion reflex. I used to get super embarrassed by minor mistakes (things like, I don’t know, forgetting to lock a public bathroom door and having someone walk in). Now those things come with a very clear sense of “gosh, what a normal-person type of mistake that millions of other people have also made, it probably didn’t even register for the other person.”
- I became much more easily moved by art. I went from being moved to tears by a poem or piece of music maybe once a year to once a week.
- I stayed much more aware of my more subtle emotional responses. I remember listening to some instrumental folk music I’d probably listened to 100 times before, suddenly noticing that the melody felt deeply nostalgic and then realizing that was two levels further out on the feelings wheel than I’d ever felt for music.
- I still felt much less of a need to be distracted or stimulated than before. I used to listen to podcasts at 2× speed whenever I wasn’t fully focused on a task (e.g. if I was doing chores or walking). Now I have no desire to do this, and when I do listen to podcasts, 2× speed feels unpleasant. Similarly, I started to dislike a lot of restaurant food, and to really enjoy very simple home-cooked meals that I would have considered too bland before.
- Friends continued to mention that my body language was different. One noted: “more upright posture, more open shoulders, more fluid and gentle.” Another described me as “‘leaving less,’ as in, spending less time ‘out of the room’ in interactions.”
These were great changes, but even better, a bunch of mindset and relational shifts also stuck around.
Mindset-wise, I guess I’d just say I got feelings-pilled.
If you’d asked me what the deal with emotions was before the retreat, I probably would have said something like:
Positive emotions are nice, although you shouldn’t let them distract you too much from your long-term goals. Painful emotions are responses to stimuli that are part of our evolutionary inheritance, but are largely maladaptive today because we’re in an environment so different from where they arose. Assuming you have good enough self-control not to act out on them, their main effect is to make you dumber, which you should mitigate by not engaging with whatever makes you emotional until you’ve chilled out.
My new view is more like the following (this is still an oversimplified strawman and reality is a lot messier):
All emotions are important and beautiful components of unconscious/intuitive cognition.‡ I do mean all, even ones that our culture really doesn’t like—for example, anger is a sign that something you care about is threatened and needs protecting, and fully welcoming it can help bring clarity about what exactly needs protecting and how. If you’re curious to learn more about the beautiful and constructive aspects of negative emotions, I recommend the book The Language of Emotions, Joe Hudson’s podcast (search for the emotion you’re interested in), or here are some specific articles summarizing his views on anger, fear, and shame. However, they sometimes create impulses to do things that aren’t actually good ideas. In the process of socializing ourselves not to follow those impulses, we’ve been trained to try to make the emotions themselves go away in painful ways, by repressing them or clenching up around them. Unintuitively, repression and clenching actually cause the emotions to “stick around” in some sort of partially metabolized state, which has negative side-effects, like anxiety, thought-looping, numbness, blind spots, impaired reasoning, bringing up secondary emotions as defenses, etc. A different way of working with these emotions, that’s better for you in the long term, is to figure out how to welcome and accept them. This eventually leads to them feeling less bad, and giving you more impulses that are good ideas and fewer bad ones. But it’s not as simple as “just stop repressing.” Welcoming emotions requires fully feeling them; if they’re strong, that can be too overwhelming to work unless you have a deep felt sense of safety. That safety can be provided in many different ways, e.g. a therapist, friends, some kinds of meditation, etc. If you don’t have access to enough safety, repressing or clenching temporarily might be your best option.
The main thing that changed my view here was spending days at the retreat paying close attention to my own emotional experience. For example, near the beginning of the retreat, one of the facilitators claimed that thought-looping was usually a sign of a stuck or repressed emotion. I was really skeptical, but then I had several very clear experiences of thought-looping around something, going off to a private place to cry about it, and immediately stopping the thought spiral and relating to it differently.§ It was a lot easier to see this clearly on the retreat than in normal life: doing lots of meditation both made it easier to notice my own thought processes, and also faster to “unstick” or welcome the underlying emotions.
Similarly, I’d ended a long-term relationship right before the retreat, which I’d felt a lot of internal conflict and self-doubt about. My therapist had often told me things like “if you felt enough of your feelings you’d feel clear about this decision,” which sounded crazy to me at the time (uh, it’s a really hard call and there are a lot of tradeoffs that I don’t know how to weigh!). But on the last day of the retreat I noticed that I felt much clearer that a lot of the things I didn’t like about that relationship were real and important, and felt much more confident in my choice to end it.
Of course, I’d already heard plenty of people say things like “you can’t make this kind of decision logically, you have to listen to your heart,” but I never really understood what they were talking about. In retrospect, I just didn’t have the ability to do the “listen to your heart” internal move, because the pipes were clogged by too much repressed gunk. I wish those soundbites had included a second sentence like “of course listening to your heart can be much easier said than done, so if it feels hard or unclear for you, here are some first steps to get started with, and be prepared for it to take you many hours (or years) to figure out what your heart is trying to say.”
This new mindset really shifted the way I related to my emotions. While I still often experience them as painful, it’s much less than I used to, and now I start from the assumption that they’re not “actually” painful or bad, and that the best thing to do with them is to figure out how to accept them, welcome them, and find the wisdom or beauty in them. Overall this feels a lot better—when I run into something stressful or hard, instead of just flailing around not knowing what to do about it, it feels more like I have some kind of sense of direction or overall orientation, which is really comforting and grounding.
I also noticed that a lot of these ways of thinking about emotional difficulty within myself were also useful metaphors for thinking about emotional dynamics between people. Just like resisting your own emotions is a common coping strategy but ultimately doesn’t work very well, resisting other people’s emotions is also a really common but damaging pattern, which I would guess is very involved in a lot of (most?) relational damage. For example, I realized that a big part of the root problem of the relationship that I had just ended was that both of us were missing the capacity to accept a bunch of each other’s emotions, which was something I hadn’t been able to articulate clearly at the time. I now think practices explicitly focused on welcoming each other’s emotions are a really valuable tool for nurturing relationships over time.
In fact, I started to notice that a lot of sticky problems—both in my personal life, but also the type that I ran into as a manager—were partly downstream of people resisting emotions of one type or another. Alice is hard to communicate with, because she frequently monologues about unimportant details, because she wants to prove she understands everything, because she has a bunch of stuck fear/resentment about not being taken seriously. Bob and Clara have a persistent disagreement about strategy, because Clara doesn’t care enough about Bob’s opinion to engage, because Bob was cruel to her once; Clara never complained because she’s avoiding the feeling of having been hurt by it. Dave’s team is underperforming but Erin, the exec sponsor, doesn’t want to manage him out, because she’s scared of the conflict and of making Dave’s reports, who like him, angry.
Of course, just because a problem is caused by stuck emotions doesn’t mean that unsticking them is the best way to solve it; but it was still a really useful lens to view what was going on.
The other shift that stuck around was in my relationships. I made a huge improvement in my friendships, which a few different things contributed to:
- I felt a lot clearer about what I wanted from close friendships—mainly, a strong felt sense of safety and care—and that this felt like a big missing piece in my life.
- I also started getting better at feeling those. With a lot of my shame reflex dissolved, I felt generally safer with people overall—my intuitive predictions about how they’d respond to things I did felt more positive. I also got a lot better at noticing and appreciating the different ways people expressed care for me.
- Because I felt generally safer, I also got more willing to take risks or be vulnerable, which helped build closeness faster. A friend reflected that I seemed more courageous.
This also meant that my “friend type” changed—I cared more about emotional connection and less about intellectual connection. Luckily, even though I live in a really heady milieu, I was still able to find a lot of big-hearted people and gradually build up a set of close friends who are wonderful at both. So far, this is the change I’m the happiest about.
My dating life changed a bunch as well:
- I started being able to feel much more strongly attracted to people. For example, later that year, I got a crush on someone that was stronger than any crush I’d had in high school.
- I had a run of anxious-attachment-ish experiences, which I’d never had before, although they got way milder over time.‖ I’m not totally sure what was up with this; I think it partly had to do with calibrating to the new level of attraction I could feel.
- The extra safety and courage I mentioned above were helpful in dating too: I got much more comfortable with communicating tricky things or being in weird situations (e.g. ones outside the standard script of “meet someone on an app, go on some dates, become exclusive”). A friend commented that it seemed like I had more degrees of freedom.
These changes have been really wonderful overall, but also kind of intense. Becoming much more emotionally aware solved a bunch of my problems, like wanting deeper friendships or not understanding why my relationship was unsatisfying. But it turns out winning that boss fight just made all the enemies level up. My old problems got replaced with horrible new problems, like that sometimes my newly deep friends and I would have conflicts, or that I would be more into the person I was dating than they were into me.
The part of my life where I feel the most mixed about the impact is work. About six months after my retreat, I got a pretty bad case of burnout and left my role managing a big team for a significantly less ambitious IC role. I don’t think the retreat was the main cause of my burnout—the main cause was that I didn’t have enough background in the area I was managing to have the type of impact that I wanted to, with a side helping of some difficult working relationships. But it turns out my new psyche had also gotten worse at repressing or avoiding emotion, and that made everything harder.
I think of repression as the emotional equivalent of technical debt or deferred maintenance: a way of avoiding an immediate cost, in this case feeling uncomfortable feelings, in exchange for paying a higher cost later to clean it up.¶ Like tech debt or deferred maintenance, sometimes repression or avoidance can be the right strategy, if you’ll have more resources to deal with the problem later! My role exposed me to a constant stream of low-grade painful things, like people being disappointed or annoyed at me, me being disappointed or annoyed at other people, projects not going well, office snacks being replaced with worse snacks, and so on. With less ability to avoid or repress, I had to feel more of them fully as they came up, and that was really draining. It also required enough time and attention that I had to defer a bunch of maintenance on my personal life.
Even now that I feel like my energy levels have mostly recovered, I’m still not sure whether I want to go back to the center of the vortex. It’s not just that I would have to start deferring maintenance again; I’m also much more aware of a lot of maintenance I’d already deferred and really want to clean up—things like investing in friendships, like I mentioned above.
If I were only working toward my personal growth and happiness, I’d say this change was clearly good. My life feels much richer and more whole, and taking a less ambitious role forced me to release some layers of ego attachment to having a “shiny” job or career, which was deeply freeing. But… I also care a lot about my impact on the world, and I think the next few years will probably be a critical period where my work could be especially high-leverage. From that perspective, it’s sad to feel resistant to maxing out on work right now.
I feel pretty unsure about what to take away from all this. Here are some disconnected reflections.
- I would never have said that I had a problem with self-compassion or self-esteem before doing this retreat, because I was able to say the right self-compassionate words when I directly asked myself things. But from my point of view afterwards, it’s clear that I did have a big self-compassion problem; even though I could say the words, I wasn’t really feeling them or acting like they were true. Having the actual embodied experience and habits of self-compassion is the closest I’ve come in real life to The Vitamin.✻✻ “My deepest darkest fantasy is that I collapse on the street and I am rushed to the hospital. They perform a bunch of tests and find out I am severely deficient in some kind of vitamin. Then I start taking the vitamin and I become the happiest cleverest person alive because all my problems were caused by this one deficiency."
- Jhourney is doing something really cool and is way better than most/all other meditation retreats on several axes (amount of support available, pedagogical excellence). That said, you probably shouldn’t expect to have this life-changing of an experience; I would guess I had something like a 95th-percentile-plus outcome in terms of long-term effects. I still think it’s awesome as an introduction to both meditation and embodied emotional awareness.
- I feel like I got really lucky on timing: if the retreat had ended one day earlier, I don’t think I would have gotten to the life-changing part. That freaks me out a lot.
- At first I concluded from this that I should get super into meditation. Then I did two more retreats (another Jhourney retreat and a Dustin DiPerna introduction to mahamudra retreat) and neither one of them had nearly as large of an effect. The second Jhourney retreat was really helpful in reinforcing my daily practice, and I do think a daily meditation practice was helpful in stabilizing the changes that became permanent, but I’m not even totally sure about that.
- Jhanas are definitely real. Also, it was kind of wild hearing somebody tell me something that sounded totally fake (“achieve bliss on demand using nothing but your brain”) and then back it up with an 80-page engineering manual and meticulous data on success rates. Overall this made me much more likely to believe that various other types of “woo” phenomena are real, at least in the sense that people are accurately describing their experiences. (I still believe the universe runs on physics, but a lot of woo things have physics-compatible explanations, especially if you buy that people’s intuitions can sometimes do spooky things.)
- I described this as “chang[ing] my personality” in the title, but I don’t want to oversell the magnitude. I do have a lot of conversations where someone asks me “so how come you’re so <like X>” and I tell them “I did this weird meditation retreat a couple years ago.” But also, there are a lot of different aspects of personality, and even though a bunch of those completely changed for me, most of them are still the same, so I’m still very similar to my past self in an absolute sense.
- What flags should tip you off that you should maybe be staring into your emotions more? Here are some things that I think might be clues (a lot of which described me, in retrospect):
  - You often get stressed and stuck in thought loops
  - You’re easily annoyed or frustrated
  - You have too much scarcity mindset—you often worry this is the best job or relationship or whatever that you’ll ever have, in a way that’s not justified by the fundamentals
  - You seek out a lot of distraction or stimulation; it’s not very enjoyable to just exist in the present
  - You feel a lot of internal conflict or uncertainty about what you want
  - There are parts of yourself that you really don’t like or wish were different
  - You feel some missing depth, richness, or meaning in your life but can’t put your finger exactly on what
I’m also just left with a lot of questions:
- Was this set of experiences… good?
  - I’m really confident that it will be good for my own long-term life happiness. But the timing meant that I feel less willing to sign up for intense work during what might be one of the most critical periods for humanity, which feels bad even if the change was positive. I feel kind of unsure how to balance these.
  - My shoulder Jeremy Bentham says I should have just kept playing push-pin. My shoulder, uh… deva? says if I follow my heart my impact on the world will be higher, even if it’s less predictable or legible. I don’t really feel like I buy either of these.
- It seemed like before this I was in a personality attractor basin with a lot of numbing and repression. How exactly did this happen, and more importantly, how can I/we avoid having it happen to as many people in the future?
  - I have some sort of fuzzy leads on this (e.g. when I was young it was really easy to make me cry so I got picked on a lot in school), but not a particularly complete picture (e.g.: does this happen to everyone who has that experience? If not, what makes the difference?)
- Mechanistically, what did the elephant meditation do? Why did it work so suddenly and create such a lasting shift? (Was that in fact what was responsible for most of the lasting shift? How much of it was instead about what I learned at a cognitive level?)
- Is the glowy, “easier to be the essence of Ben” state that I ended up in right afterwards something that can be stabilized (or have aspects stabilized)? If so, how?
- More generally—where do I go from here? I’m convinced that doing the right type of introspection or meditation type thing for a week can unlock amazing things, and it feels like there are more amazing things out there to unlock, but I wish I had a better way of navigating towards them than semi-randomly trying things.
A draft reader asked what I would tell my past self, or someone like me, about this arc. Ultimately, I’d boil it down to:
- There are probably a bunch of feelings that you’re refusing to welcome into your experience, and this is probably involved in a bunch of the things that are unsatisfying about your life and relationships.
- You can improve this by gradually, in a safe context, paying loving attention to the uncomfortable parts, from a place of wanting to know and care for yourself rather than to fix yourself.
- When that’s hard, remember that it’s not your fault. You’re just the rider; elephants gonna elephant. Let it go where it needs to and keep loving it as best you can.
Thanks to Alex Allain, Catherine Olsson, Elena Lake, Jess Ocean, Rahul Dalal, and Sasha Chapin for feedback on a draft of this post.

Microsoft Admits Some Emails Bounced During Global Outlook Outage, Reveals New Failures
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
19:51:00 UTC
04:51:00 KST

Microsoft Admits Some Emails Bounced During Global Outlook Outage, Reveals New Failures (neowin.net) 6
			
		 	
				After millions lost access to Microsoft 365 services (including Outlook) on November 12, Microsoft "has published the final post-incident report," writes Neowin, "and it reveals that some emails were never delivered to their intended recipients."
"The report also details previously undisclosed failures affecting Microsoft's email security features..."
   Microsoft confirmed that all queued enterprise emails were eventually delivered and that it found no enterprise messages that were permanently lost. However, it is not the same with Outlook consumer users. The company acknowledged that some delayed messages destined for consumer mailboxes that had been inactive for more than 30 days expired, and senders received non-delivery reports. Microsoft did not reveal how many emails were affected or how many users received non-delivery reports.
The final report also reveals that the outage disrupted several important email security capabilities in Microsoft Defender for Office. For example, the Zero-hour auto purge (ZAP) feature, which automatically removes malicious emails after delivery, was delayed because updated threat intelligence was unavailable. This means affected messages may not have been removed as quickly as usual. Microsoft also confirmed that users experienced problems opening URLs protected by Safe Links. Depending on their organization's security policies, some users may have encountered a "Skip verification" option when Safe Links attempted to check a URL...
Microsoft also acknowledged that its first customer notification arrived approximately 20 minutes after the widespread impact began. It plans to improve automated outage notifications by the end of 2026 to improve communication. 
"Microsoft eventually restored the services about 12 hours later..."

Assume People Are Not Interested in Your Blog Post
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
19:41:30 UTC
04:41:30 KST

Assume People Are Not Interested in Your Blog Post
Write for your reader: tell them up front who this is for, so they can decide whether to keep reading.
If you do not write for other people, stop reading this now.
When I was at Snowflake, one of the coolest things that everybody did was this etiquette thing. The first sentence of each email would be “If you don’t develop in this specific part of the code, you can stop reading.” or “If you’re not part of the finance org, you can stop reading.”
This made me think a lot about what it means to write for other people. You need to be subservient to your audience. Similarly, thinking about how much space you take is very important. For Slack/Teams threads, you would start with a 3- to 5-line introduction to your topic, and then people could find more details inside the threads.
When I see people writing 2,000 words with content about things that may not apply to everybody, it just makes me sad. Especially when they’re not brief enough. It’s a waste of your readers’ eyes.
So please be kind to your readers.

What Happened to Silicon Valley?
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
19:20:15 UTC
04:20:15 KST

On October 16, 2026, I delivered the second Annual Margaret Levi Lecture in Political Economy in conjunction with the AI and History conference at Johns Hopkins University in Baltimore. This is my original lecture as written, which incorporates material from many pieces I’ve written over the years. As delivered, I departed significantly from the script, as you can see from the embedded video below or the transcript that you can see at the link above.
It’s an honor to give a lecture in Margaret Levi’s name. I’ve been asked to reflect on the changes in the political economy of Silicon Valley throughout my 40-plus year career in and about it, and I can’t imagine a better framing for my remarks than Margaret’s vision.
Margaret understood that advances in our ability to work together are essential to a better future. Cooperation is the human superpower. She also asks what happens when the systems of cooperation we have constructed over time stop holding up their end of the bargain.
That is very much the question that Silicon Valley is facing today. An industry that pioneered advances in collective intelligence at scale used to engender love from its customers because of the way it solved new problems in their lives. It now too often inspires fear and loathing. How did things go so wrong?
The web I thought we were building in 1993
But let me start with my own history. I started my company more than 40 years ago with a $500 initial investment. We took in no venture capital, and we built a business that now generates hundreds of millions of dollars in profitable annual revenue, funded by customers who love our products and pay us for them. We got there the slow way, through steady organic growth. That is quite anathema to the rush to the top of whatever appears to be the latest hill that seems to characterize Silicon Valley today. Even when climbing that hill traps a company at a local maximum, the hope is to get to payday before the industry moves on to the next hill.
My original corporate goal was stated as simply “Interesting work for interesting people.” That evolved into two principles: “Change the world by spreading the knowledge of innovators,” and “Create more value than you capture.” Those principles shaped not only my company but what I expected the internet to be. When we launched GNN, the Global Network Navigator, in 1993, it was the first web portal and the first ad-supported site on the World Wide Web, and for two years it was the place people went to find other sites. What we built was a machine for pointing you at the best of what other people had made, and then getting out of your way.
Even our idea of advertising was not what you think of today. It was inspired by direct mail advertising, where you’d read articles in a print magazine or look at accompanying ads, and then you’d call an 800 number or circle a number on a “bingo card” that you’d send in to the publisher so that the advertiser could send you a brochure. I realized that the web would be a good replacement for that awkward system, that every company ought to have its own website that provided more information about its products and services. What we invented was not the “grab your attention” kind of advertising. It was all about utility, and sharing information that customers really wanted and needed. In short, it was the commercial web, which no one any longer thinks of as advertising, even though it is the most durable and useful kind.
The commercial web was only a small part of what the web became. The web was the playground of amateurs, not entrepreneurs. It was a marvelous collective work of human imagination, creativity, sharing, and ultimately (though that became toxic only later), competition for attention. Our hand-curated catalog of the best websites was soon surpassed by algorithmic solutions like those provided by Google. The amount of content produced was far larger than purely human curation could manage. It was an early example of what Richard Sutton was later to call “the bitter lesson” in his analysis of AI training.
In my 2005 essay “What Is Web 2.0?” I described how the companies that survived the dotcom bust had all, in one way or another, figured out how to help people navigate the resulting information explosion at scale. As Herbert Simon noted in 1971, an abundance of information leads to a scarcity of attention. In the face of increasing information abundance, he predicted that we would use machines to help us better allocate our time and attention. And so it has transpired: Information has become so abundant that it defies manual curation. Instead, powerful, proprietary algorithmic systems use the data they collect to match users with the answers, news, entertainment, products, applications, and services they seek. Today’s AI is in many ways a continuation of this arc.
I called this “harnessing collective intelligence.” All of it was, in economic terms, a set of remarkable advances in networked, data-enabled market coordination. It felt like a public good even when it was privately owned.
Hal Varian, formerly Google’s chief economist, once told me that only about 6% of Google search result pages carry any advertising or are otherwise monetized by Google. The other 94% were served up by search in a glorious demonstration of abundance, the surplus of value created over value captured. And while many of the pages themselves were commercial, even more were the product of the joyful exuberance of humanity, people creating and sharing for the joy of it. Google’s algorithm matched that creation to the people who wanted it, with no price involved anywhere in the transaction. Forty thousand times a second, Google’s organic search algorithms were performing the coordination that twentieth-century economists thought could only be done by self-interested actors haggling over prices. And for Google’s first decade and a half, the priced market of advertising ran alongside organic search as a sidecar, not the engine.
That’s the Silicon Valley I signed up for. Some of this is nostalgia. The early web had plenty wrong with it, its share of greedy hustlers and hypesters. But value creation for both users and suppliers was the winning strategy. Companies won by solving real problems for their customers. Investors won when the company delivered on their promises. Unfortunately, that didn’t last.
The turn to the dark side
Google, a company once known for “Don’t be evil,” now faces the same surveillance-capitalism charges as Facebook, a company that never made any such promise. On Google, advertising now pushes organic search far down the page. Especially on mobile, you often scroll through pages of ads from national chains before you reach the site that has the best answer to your question. And in today’s AI-mode search, even the content is served up directly by Google, with far less traffic sent on to the sites that provide it. Likewise, Amazon, which used to call itself the most customer-centric company on earth, now puts sixteen ads and four organic results on a typical search results page. Sometimes, it may take scrolling past three screens of results to reach the first organic search result. And by “the first organic search result,” I mean “the best guess of the company’s smartest algorithms for what the user is actually looking for.” In former times, that was the top result. No more.
The reason isn’t that good people turned bad. It’s in the overriding economic incentives of today’s shareholder capitalism. The extractive behavior the tech giants exhibit has been the norm for modern companies since Milton Friedman reset its objective in 1970, in a sentence that became doctrine: “The social responsibility of business is to increase its profits.” Harvard Business School, through the work of Meckling and Jensen, enshrined that view not only into corporate law but executive compensation. Whatever people’s original motives, the logic of the market eventually prevails, except in extraordinary cases. In his recent book Incorruptible, Eric Ries describes a set of institutions, norms, and guardrails that can help to safeguard the values of a company, but you can count on your fingers and toes the high profile companies that have successfully deployed them to resist the market. Even tax policy rewards capital appreciation by taxing it at a much lower rate than labor income.
Our corporations and the financial markets that govern them and reward them are a kind of machine, what the science fiction writer Charlie Stross calls a “slow AI.” We built it. We set its objective function to reward shareholders and ignore everyone else. And then we tell everyone that it is a marvelous self-correcting system. This is an illusion. As I put it in “We Have Already Let the Genie Out of the Bottle,” an essay I wrote for a Rockefeller Foundation workshop on regulating AI:
Attempts at governance are futile until we recognize that we have built a machine and set it on its course. Instead, we pretend that the market is a natural phenomenon best left alone, and we fail to hold its mechanism designers to account. We need to tear down and rebuild that machine, reprogramming it so that human flourishing, not corporate profit, becomes its goal.
If the problem were just villains, you could fix things by firing them or shaming them or suing them or otherwise holding them to account. But if the problem is the design of the entire system, everyone is eventually incentivized to seek the same result. The machine rewards outsized growth of revenue and profit and brutally punishes any slowdown. Not only that, stock options tie every employee’s fortune to that reward.
Criticizing a company for turning extractive isn’t really about the values of a company or the character of its executives and employees, it’s about the incentives that have been given to them.
Rising tide rents and robber baron rents
Silicon Valley is a mirror of what’s wrong with our economy and our corporate governance, not the cause of it, and not even the worst exemplar. Tobacco, oil, pharma companies, and far too much of Wall Street vie for that spot. But Silicon Valley is a great place to study what went wrong, because companies rise and fall so quickly, making them the equivalent of laboratory mice or flatworms, where patterns can be quickly identified and followed across generations.
A few years ago, I found a useful language for describing what I was seeing in the theory of economic rents.
The essential idea is that when the reward accruing to an actor is larger than their contribution to value creation, the difference is rent. To the classical economists, to Smith and Ricardo and Mill, living in a world of inherited privilege, rents were not a temporary aberration on the way to equilibrium. They were both a persistent consequence and a persistent source of inequality. Joseph Schumpeter had a different idea, that disproportionate profits can be a reward for innovation.
