The Birds of GTA 6: A Field Guide to Vice City and Leonida's Wildlife
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
19:10:34 UTC
04:10:34 KST

I finished collecting the bricks of cocaine and landed my bullet-ridden seaplane at the dock. It was time for birdwatching.
A few minutes after midnight on Thursday morning, I joined millions of other gamers for the release of Grand Theft Auto VI, the latest in the smash video game series from Rockstar Games. The new installment is already a worldwide sensation, earning more than $1.8 billion in revenue the day it was released. 
Grand Theft Auto, or GTA, games are famous for their “open worlds,” which allow players to explore massive, intricately detailed versions of America. Much of that exploration is focused on mayhem and chaos: The story missions usher players through criminal underworlds, and players are at any time free to steal cars, rob stores and pedestrians, and initiate anarchy. 
But GTA 6 has more going on than just criminality—it’s also a world filled with wildlife. Previous GTA games were set in fictionalized versions of New York City and Hollywood, but the new one returns to Vice City, its version of Miami, which was also the setting for 2002’s installment. Unlike that game, however, GTA 6’s South Florida is complete with dozens of species of birds, reptiles, amphibians, fish, mammals, and other creatures. The in-game Progress menu tracks all kinds of items to find, including 338 vehicles, 205 weapons, and, of primary interest to me, at least 60 bird species.
I made it my mission to find them all as quickly as possible.
(Side note: Pre-release marketing for GTA 6 touted 62 bird species, but the official birds Progress screen only lists 60, with a handful of others listed on the hunting screen.)
I am one hour in and I’ve found 16/60 birds https://t.co/81x42O1BuY
— The Birdist (@TheBirdist) November 19, 2026
You begin the game as Jason, a low-level criminal working to raise money to support his young family. You’re equipped with zoom binoculars before the very first mission—tracking drug runners through a swamp and escaping with the seaplane—and I used them immediately, stopping mid-lurk to scan some Double-crested Cormorants on a dock. (Distracted from the job at hand to look at birds—just like real life!) They looked like Double-crested Cormorants!
After the first mission the player is free to explore the entirety of the map, stealing cars, robbing stores, or just birding wherever they want. The game makes birding easy: Looking at any bird through the in-game binoculars gives players the option to hold a button to “study” it, thereby adding it to the Progress menu. Simply standing on the back deck of Jason’s first safehouse yields a bunch of species like Brown Pelican and Laughing Gull. It’s just like being in South Florida!
Looking south from the back deck, I spotted another bird perched on an adjacent dock, with a few people gathered at the end. Looking through my binoculars I spotted my first Herring Gull—and noticed that the people, a woman and three men, were fully and visibly naked. Jason apparently lives next to a nudist resort. It’s just like being in South Florida!
Two down, 58 to go. I found a few more species right in Jason’s backyard, including American Crow, House Sparrow, and South Florida-appropriate Monk Parakeets, before venturing out into the huge map. My strategy for finding birds in GTA 6 was the same as finding birds in real life: look in as many habitats as I could find. I started by “borrowing” a fanboat off a nearby dock and heading back to the swampy areas near the seaplane mission. There were tons of birds across the open water and in the marsh: Caspian Terns, Mottled Ducks, Sanderlings, Spotted Sandpipers, Great Blue Herons, Glossy and White Ibis, and … flocks of Whooping Cranes?
incredibly healthy populations of critically-endangered Whooping Cranes across Vice City. Huge if true. https://t.co/doe6oMOvBX
— The Birdist (@TheBirdist) November 19, 2026
In fact, I found a number of avian inaccuracies in just my first few hours of playtime. Certain species like Whooping Crane and Red-cockaded Woodpecker are common throughout the map, despite being endangered and threatened, respectively, in real life. Golden Eagles are extremely rare in Florida, but I found one soaring over the Mt. Kalaga area (mountains, one may point out, aren’t found in Florida either). Some species have confusing naming quirks—the chickadee is Black-capped instead of Carolina, and there’s a “New World Oriole” instead of a Baltimore Oriole—but those changes are likely due to the fact that Carolina and Baltimore don’t exist in the GTA universe.
Though some of the inaccuracies may feel like a letdown in a game that is being hailed as the Greatest of All Time, I think it’s important to remember that it’s also game where you can get a ride from a dog driving a jet ski. Grand Theft Auto games are meant to be fun, not science. Plus, despite some of the misfires, the avifauna feels well thought-out. The map is true to the ecological boundaries of South Florida, with herons, gulls, and other waterbirds in the south while birds like Red-bellied Woodpecker, Wood Stork, and American Robin are found farther north. Birders know that certain exotic species have made their home in tropical South Florida, including Spot-breasted Oriole and Red Junglefowl, both in the game.
That geographic fidelity is the main tip for anyone looking to check off all the game’s birds: Find their habitat. The game doesn’t tell you what species to look for, so you’ll need to cover a lot of ground to find everything. Wading birds, shorebirds, and seabirds are common in the southern coastal part of the map. A number of smaller, open-country or forest birds are found up north. Owls come out at night. Birds in zoos and domestic farmyard birds count towards the 60, too, so don’t overlook those areas.
Wild Boar roadkill. It’s just like birding in Florida! https://t.co/gT84IticOK
— The Birdist (@TheBirdist) November 19, 2026
The most accurate aspect is the actual landscape. Having birded extensively across South Florida myself, the habitats are immediately recognizable, from the pebbly mangrove beaches at the Keys to the grassy swamps further north. The setting feels absolutely real, from the fonts on the roadside fruit stands to the roadkill alongside the highways. (I found dead wild boar and dead deer by the side of the road, and a Turkey Vulture somehow flew into a building I was in and died at my feet.)

Dirac's Theorem at a Dinner Party
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
18:50:27 UTC
03:50:27 KST

Dirac’s Theorem at a Dinner Party
Gabriel Dirac (the famous graph theorist) was the stepson of Paul Dirac (the even more famous physicist). Paul Dirac spent the last years of his career at Florida State University, and my father took his course in General Relativity in the 1970s.
Gabriel formulated an important theorem about Hamiltonian cycles. In this post I want to give a refresher on Hamiltonian cycles, and go over a fun proof of his theorem that involves a hypothetical dinner party.
Over the course of several decades, Dirac’s theorem was improved when mathematicians formulated similar theorems that work with a broader range of graphs. In the following figure, each theorem is more general than the one to its left. Each graph meets its own theorem’s condition, but none of the ones to its left, and its Hamiltonian cycle is blue.
Why am I writing about Dirac’s Theorem? Well, I wanted to write about a simple proof relating to Hamiltonian cycles as prep for my next post, which will be about Barnette’s Conjecture, one of my favorite graph theory conjectures. It got promoted to a theorem in the 6-Oct-2026 Mathocalypse. In my next post I’ll talk more about that.
A Hamiltonian cycle is a walk along the edges of a graph that visits every vertex exactly once and ends up back where it started. A graph that has one is Hamiltonian.
Hamiltonian cycles are related to the Traveling Salesman Problem (e.g. designing delivery routes for Amazon packages). But my personal favorite application is an old-school computer graphics problem: given a triangle mesh, can you render it using a single triangle strip?
Intuitively, it should be easier to find a Hamiltonian cycle in a graph that has lots of edges relative to its vertex count. In a way, that intuition is what Dirac formalized:
If a simple graph has  vertices, and every vertex has at least  neighbors, then the graph has a Hamiltonian cycle.
The following figure shows a random graph with 10 vertices, where every vertex has exactly 5 neighbors, so the theorem guarantees a Hamiltonian cycle. On the right, the same graph has been rearranged so that its Hamiltonian cycle runs around the outside…like a circular dinner table.
1. Dinner party
To see why the theorem is true, first imagine a party where each guest is a vertex and each friendship is an edge. Friendship goes both ways, and nobody is their own friend, which is what simple means in graph theory. A Hamiltonian cycle is a way to seat everyone at a round table so that each guest sits between two friends:
If a party has  guests, and every guest is friends with at least  of the others, then the guests can sit at a round table such that each one sits between two friends.
Here’s the plan. Seat everyone in random order. Two guests who sit side by side are seatmates, and they might be friends, or strangers. Seatmates who are friends get a blue edge, and other friends get a gray edge. Seatmates who are strangers make a gap, which gets a dotted orange line. Here’s the graph from before as a party, seated in random order, with four gaps:
So, if we can find a way that always gets rid of all the gaps, then we will have shown that the theorem is true. We’ll do it with a sequence of moves somewhat akin to playing musical chairs.
2. Musical chairs
Say that strangers  and  sit side by side, with  just clockwise of . And say that somewhere else at the table, there are two more seatmates,  and , with  just clockwise of , where  is a friend of , and  is a friend of .
Here’s the move: everyone from  clockwise around to  stands up, and sits back down in the reverse order. That’s a flip. Afterward,  sits next to , and  sits next to , and those are both pairs of friends.
In the following figure, the stretch that flips is shaded blue. Drag the slider under it to watch it flip.
A flip closes the gap between  and , and opens no new gap.
Why is Lemma 1 true? Inside the stretch that flips, each guest has the same two seatmates as before, just on opposite sides. So the only seatmates who change are at the two endpoints. Before the flip, those were  and , who are strangers, and  and . After the flip, they’re  and , and  and , who are friends. (If  and  were strangers too, then the flip closes two gaps at once.)
The friendship of  and , and the friendship of  and , cross like an X. Let’s call a pair of friendships like that a crossing: one from  to , and one from  to the guest just clockwise of . Each crossing gives a flip.
The following interactive figure runs the algorithm on the party from before, starting with the seating arrangement that happens to have four gaps, and flips until there are none.
Here’s the algorithm:
seat everyone in any order
while some strangers u and v sit side by side, with v just clockwise of u:
    find a friend x of u who sits just counterclockwise of a friend y of v
    reverse the order of everyone from v around to x
So the whole proof comes down to one question. Do gaps always have corresponding crossings? In the next section we’ll answer that question.
3. Pass the potatoes
Recall that  is the number of guests at the party. Let’s write  for the number of friends of guest . Here’s what we seek to prove:
Say strangers  and  sit side by side, with  just clockwise of , and . Then somewhere at the table, a friend of  sits just counterclockwise of a friend of .
One way to see how this is true is the following thought experiment. Give a red potato to each friend of , and a Yukon Gold to each friend of . That’s  potatoes, which is at least . Then everyone with a red potato passes it one seat clockwise, like this:
After that, note that  can no longer hold any potatoes. So the other  guests hold all of the potatoes. Therefore, since there are at least  total potatoes, some guest holds two.
Let’s use  to refer to a guest with two potatoes (circled above). They have a Yukon Gold, so  is a friend of . The red one came from the guest just counterclockwise of , who is a friend of , and that’s . That’s the crossing.
Back to Dirac’s theorem. If every guest has at least  friends, then for any two strangers, , so every gap has a crossing (Lemma 2). Each flip closes a gap and opens none (Lemma 1), so the number of gaps drops with every flip, until there are none, and everyone sits between two friends.
4. References
Edgar Palmer discovered this proof in the work of Øystein Ore and wrote about it in The hidden algorithm of Ore’s theorem on Hamiltonian cycles (though he did not use dinner tables and potatoes).
Dirac’s theorem first appeared in his paper Some theorems on abstract graphs, in the Proceedings of the London Mathematical Society in 1952.
Homo sapiens. What an inventive, invincible species.
— The Fourth Doctor, The Ark in Space (1975)

Climate Change Talks Fall Apart In Final Hours Over Fossil Fuels
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
18:41:00 UTC
03:41:00 KST

Climate Change Talks Fall Apart In Final Hours Over Fossil Fuels (npr.org) 62
			
		 	
				NPR reports international climate talks "broke down in overtime hours, over the main cause of planetary warming — fossil fuels."
At Sunday's U.N. climate summit, "nearly 200 countries failed to reach an agreement on whether they should move away from energy sources like coal and natural gas."
The talks ended in a dramatic fashion, with the European Union negotiators saying they could not agree to a final agreement without mentioning the transition away from fossil fuels — the primary driver of climate change. The EU bloc then walked out of the room...  
After representatives of the EU walked out, other countries also objected — including Panama and island states such as the Marshall Islands.  The European climate chief, Wopke Hoekstra, said that when the world is heading for an overshoot of 1.5 degrees, the EU could not tolerate an agreement that does not mention fossil fuels. "That is not acceptable," Hoekstra told reporters after walking out of the talks. "We do that with a heavy heart with many other countries, but we simply cannot accept this."  
"Burning fossil fuels like oil, gas and coal make up roughly 73% of global emissions," the article notes, adding  this is the first U.N. climate summit with a scientific consensus that the planet has already warmed past the threshhold countries agreed not to pass in 2015 of 1.5 degrees Celsius (2.7 degrees Fahrenheit).

Why data centers are at the center of the Big Tech backlash
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
18:08:43 UTC
03:08:43 KST

In his new book “Hyperscale,” journalist Paris Marx investigates the impact of data centers and generative AI, taking readers inside the data centers themselves and explaining how computing infrastructure that goes back decades recently became a political landmine.
The book is well-timed, to put it mildly, as data center backlash seems to have been one of the motivators for the massive blue wave seen in the recent midterm elections. And those debates are likely to continue as Democrats wield more governmental power.
In an interview with TechCrunch, Marx described the data center buildout as “fundamentally unsustainable” and “completely out of line with a livable planet,” while also arguing that these facilities make for an obvious target amidst broader anger at the tech industry. After all, if a data center is being built in your community, “it’s very physical, it’s very material.”
“It doesn’t matter what side of the political spectrum that you’re on,“ Marx said. “You are going to feel the effects of that in your quality of life, in your power bill, potentially in your access to water, potentially in what you hear around your community, if these air conditioning units on the roofs of data centers are making a lot of noise.”
He also suggested that data center opposition is only the most visible piece of a wider movement targeting “the physical manifestations of the tech industry, […] whether it’s the opposition to Flock cameras rolling out across the United States, or the smart glasses that Meta is trying to put on people’s faces, or even the self-driving cars that Waymo is rolling out.”
During our conversation (which I’ve edited for length and clarity), Marx and I discussed data centers’ environmental impact, how tech companies are responding to community resistance, and what it’s actually like inside a data center.
I liked your description of trying to get access to the inside of a “hyperscale” data center and then eventually taking a tour [of one]. Could you talk a little bit more about what that experience was like? How did you get in? What surprised you when you actually got in?
Getting inside of a hyperscale data center was not the easiest task in the world. I spoke to some researchers who have been doing research on data centers for a number of years longer than I had, and they told me that before the generative AI moment, and before all the attention that has been put on data centers recently, that it was quite a bit easier to do so.
I sent emails to a number of companies asking if I could get inside, often didn’t get a response, other times got a no. I ended up, after visiting the outsides of some data centers in places like northern Virginia and Ireland and spots like that, I actually went down to New Zealand to go inside of a data center itself. And the thing that struck me most was the level of security.
Obviously, you would expect that this is an infrastructure that is really securitized, because the data inside needs to be kept safe, obviously [they] have these big fences and all the security cameras around the data centers. But to go inside, I had to send my passport in advance. Then when I got there, they had to check the passport, and then as we were going through the data center itself, every single door in the data center was locked unless you had a key card to unlock it.
Then, when you get inside of the server room itself, where all this computer hardware is stored in tall racks that look like cabinets — you can imagine, in your mind, that this would be kind of a sinister location, with some eerie lighting or something like that. But in truth, it’s just quite utilitarian. It’s a warm room with some air conditioning blasting into the servers from vents that are nearby.
It was really loud as well, because you have all these fans running from all these servers, it was really kind of piercing. To be inside of this room, if you were going to be in there for a while, you’d probably want earplugs or something like that.
Before I read your book, I thought of data centers as something that’s important, but a little bit boring. How did you decide they were interesting and rich enough to sustain an entire book?
I felt the same. I was aware that data centers had been out there, but it was not something that I was paying particularly close attention to. But then there were a number of stories that of tipped me off to the fact that there might be more here, and that it was worth looking deeper into what was going on — the first being hearing about the water pressures created by data centers in Chile around the capital of Santiago, where companies like Google and Microsoft were building data centers, and communities were concerned about their access to water and started organizing around that.
And then hearing about how much electricity data centers were consuming in Ireland, to the point that now they consume more than 20% of the country’s electricity, and that was obviously pushing up power prices. It was putting pressures on the grid, such that the grid operator actually put a moratorium in place for grid connections for data centers around the Dublin area, back in 2022. This was even before the generative AI moment that caused a surge in construction of data centers.
And then the other one was in The Dalles, Oregon, where Google had actually built its first company-owned data centers about 20 years back. In that case, its water deal was coming up for renewal. It wanted to build more data centers there, so it needed more water. And when the residents started asking, “How much water is Google actually using?” a journalist at The Oregonian went to the city and essentially asked, “Hey, how much water is Google using? Our readers would like to know this,” he was hit with a lawsuit instead of being given that information. Because Google did not want it to be public. And then, of course, when it did come out about a year later, Google revealed that it was using 29% of the city’s water, which seemed quite significant.
That was around when ChatGPT was being released and generative AI was starting to take off. It felt like, okay, there are these pockets of places where a lot of data centers have already been constructed, where they are putting pressures on the communities that are causing people to respond, and it felt like that was going to start coming to a lot more communities. It was a good time to really start to dig in and pay attention.
You don’t just talk about the data centers’ environmental impact, but also, they [become] your way in to talk about this broader tech moment. They represent to you — particularly the hyperscale data centers — represent to you how these tech companies are doing business now.
Absolutely. The hyperscale data centers and just the scale of the construction — whether it’s the the size of the actual facilities themselves or the number that these companies are trying to build out, just feels fundamentally unsustainable.
They’re just demanding too much from the communities where they’re being located, whether that’s because of electricity or because of water use or what have you, or just the fact that they’re building out so many, that when you think about the cumulative impact and you look at the demand for water, but particularly for energy from fossil fuels, that means that it’s just completely out of line with a livable planet and an amount of computation that feels like it makes any degree of sense for us to have constructed.
I think it really goes back to the hubris of the tech industry and of these major tech companies and the tech billionaires that run them, and the fact that they have these particular visions of the future that are based on fantasies, but also on expanding their own power and influence over the world, and they don’t seem to mind that everyone else then has to pay the cost that comes with their ambitions and what they are seeking to do.
And I feel like it’s become particularly egregious with generative AI and the data centers to power them, where many of the benefits that were supposed to come of generative AI do not appear to have materialized — whether those are the economic benefits, the productivity benefits, but also the the benefits of delivery of services like healthcare and education to the public, as we heard Sam Altman saying a lot in the in the early days of ChatGPT.
Paired with that, there has been this massive environmental cost […] but there’s also this massive and growing social cost, when you think about the mental health impacts of chatbot dependence, of all the false information that pervades social media platforms now, of the deepfake crisis, and I could go on and on and on.
It’s not surprising that there has been backlash. But I have been surprised at how widespread it’s been, and also how bipartisan — you talk about Ron DeSantis and Bernie Sanders both being opposed to data centers. Why do you think it’s become such a unifying issue?
Because it’s not an abstract issue, right? I think even when we think about generative AI or social media or something like that, [it] can feel abstract. It’s a service that you access from your phone or from your computer. It’s not something that is tangible in the world.
But when a data center is coming to set up shop in your community, it’s very physical, it’s very material. It doesn’t matter what side of the political spectrum that you’re on. You are going to feel the effects of that in your quality of life, in your power bill, potentially in your access to water, potentially in what you hear around your community, if these air conditioning units on the roofs of data centers are making a lot of noise.
I think that there was already a pre-existing frustration with the tech industry that came from the quality of the products, the effects that they’re having on our society and our lives. For some people, [they have] frustration with the way that the tech industry has been collaborating with the Trump administration and with right-wing political groups in different parts of the world. But then you add on top of that the actual effects that come of having one of these infrastructures be planned or located in your community, and that anger gets kind of directed into that physical manifestation of this thing that you have an issue with.
Obviously, the data centers are at the forefront of this, and it’s what we pay the most attention to. But we also see this increasingly with other aspects of the tech industry, whether it’s the opposition to Flock cameras rolling out across the United States, or the smart glasses that Meta is trying to put on people’s faces, or even the self-driving cars that Waymo is rolling out.
There’s a lot of frustration with the physical manifestations of the tech industry, because that is something that can really be felt more directly, but can also be challenged more directly. You can delete an app on your phone, but what difference does that really make to the company? It doesn’t often seem like very much.
The tech companies have reacted to this backlash in a few different ways. One is that Microsoft, Amazon, and OpenAI have all talked about [their commitment] that they’re not going to use NDAs anymore in their negotiations with local governments. How big a difference do you think that will make?
Not a huge difference.
I do think it’s going to be good that it will be easier for people to access information on these projects that are being located in their communities. But part of the reason why we’re seeing these shifts is exactly in response to that opposition.
We’re seeing the tech companies propose to pay for more of the electricity infrastructure that will be required for their data centers to reduce the pressure on ratepayers, because people are angry about that. They’re promising to use different water-based cooling systems in their data centers, to say that they’re able to use less electricity.
When we look at the NDAs and the decision or the announcement that they’re not going to use those anymore, I think it’s also because it’s working against them. People know what a data center is now, and if people find out that a data center is coming to town and they have questions and they’re not getting answers, that’s only going to further fuel the opposition to those data centers.
So, it’s not really a surprise to see [the tech companies] do this, and it’s a way that they can get a positive PR thing by saying, “We’re not going to use these NDAs that are working against us anyway. “
One of the other ways they’ve responded is by saying that there’s a lot of misinformation about the environmental harms, or that they’re being overstated, or that they can mitigate them with renewable technology. How valid do you think those arguments are?
I think that the industry recognizes that there is a lot of concern and they’re trying to basically move into the discussion with narratives of their own to have people question whether data centers are really a problem or not.
I feel like we don’t see the industry doing as much on the energy side anymore, particularly on the fossil fuel side, because they realize that they have kind of lost that argument. If you look back a year and a half, two years ago, they were talking a lot about renewables, about nuclear energy, you don’t hear so much of that discourse anymore.
They have pivoted, instead, to focus on the water question, where I think that they feel that they can use more abstract national figures in order to confuse the public about what’s going on. They can say, “If you look nationally, agriculture and golf courses also use a lot of water. So you don’t need to be worried about a data center coming to tow,n because they don’t use as much, in aggregate.”
But the issue with data center water use is not a national issue. It’s a local issue. There are unquestionably going to be communities where they seek to locate data centers that are going to have plenty of water and that are not going to be concerned about a data center coming to town. But they’re also locating [data centers] in a lot of communities that are experiencing high degrees of water stress, which means that they don’t have the water to really provide to these infrastructures.
We have also seen them pivoting to move away from the evaporative cooling systems, the ones that require a lot of water — because you need to take in a certain amount of water as it gets too hot, it evaporates away, and so you can see why you need a consistent flow of water into the data center for that type of cooling system — to a closed loop system, where you basically have a certain amount of water, it stays within the data center, you keep cooling it down by using electricity to do that, and so you clearly don’t need as much water.
That is their new way to say, “Hey, don’t worry about the water use. We’re going to switch to this other system.” But the issue there, of course, is that it does require more electricity in order to keep that water cooled down, and that is often not part of what they talk about or discuss when they are proposing that as an alternative.
Then the other thing is that it’s not just water that is in those pipes or tubes going through the data center. There’s also a lot of coolant in there, a lot of chemicals, and communities where these projects are being located with closed loop cooling systems are becoming increasingly concerned about those coolants leaking out into their water supply and what that is going to mean.
In the book, you say repeatedly that you’re not against technology itself or even data centers in general, but [against] this hyperscale approach. The book is called “Hyperscale.” The last chapter is called “Human Scale.” Why do you see scale as such an important part of the problem?
Because scale is what creates the pressures, whether those are the pressures on the communities or you know the pressures on our wider electricity system and whatnot.
I don’t think that the inherent problem is data centers, because we have had data centers for a long time, and sure, there have been occasional concerns about specific projects, but data centers as a whole have not been an issue that the public has felt merited this degree of attention. So that is why I feel the hyperscale data center, and particularly the acceleration that we’ve seen in the expansion of the size of these hyperscale data centers, but also the number of data centers that these companies are building out, is notable in this moment.
Linked to that is not just the scale of the demand created by these hyperscale data centers, but also, what they are supposed to be powering? That is where the other disconnect lies. They’re demanding a lot, but if we were actually getting something good for it, this might be justified. I think that for a lot of people, and I feel like we see this reflected in polling, they’re not actually seeing the benefits from generative AI.
So then the question becomes: “Okay, we’re expending all of these resources, we’re pushing up emissions such that climate change is going to be accelerated just to build out all these data centers to meet the ambitions of generative AI that the tech companies have, but then we’re not actually getting much benefit out of that technology. So what are we actually doing here?”
One other thing that really struck a chord with me was your description about how tech executives often talk about the future as if there’s [just one] inevitable future. The only thing that’s in question is how quickly we arrive there [and] who’s going to make the most money off of it.
This is so important for me, because I do feel like how we think about the future then shapes decisions that we make. The tech billionaires, they see the future in a certain way […] and I think that they have been very successful in getting us to buy into their vision of the future for the past several decades. But that does not mean that that is the only way that the future has to be.
We can imagine a very different kind of future, and that would mean that we place our resources in different places and actually develop technology in a different way, toward different outcomes, with different incentives driving that work.
There are interesting conversations happening in different parts of the world right now around things like digital sovereignty, getting off of these technologies from the U.S. tech companies. But in some cases, those conversations are quickly captured by industry in those different parts of the world to say, “Okay, we want to get off U.S. technology, but let’s have our local companies do effectively the same thing, and just let them do the same exploitative practices and make the money that the U.S. tech companies would be making.” And I don’t think that that is the right way forward.
I also think that there is a role for public sector development of technology in a way that we’ve eschewed or even looked at skeptically for the past number of decades, because the idea was that technology had to be left to the private sector. What we’re starting to see in Europe is a bit more funding of public technology, in order to develop the services that government itself depends on, and I think that that is a good first step.
But especially when we’re looking at the Microsoft outage that happened just recently, and we’re looking at Google threatening schools with their access to Google search if they don’t accept Google AI tool, I think that there’s a real moment right now to put resources into developing something different, that doesn’t come from these major tech companies, and that hopefully can have much more public benefit than what these companies now provide us.

Harvard CS 2881R
Everyone who wants to be considered for the course must fill out the Fall 2026 course registration form.
In-person attendance will be mandatory for students enrolled in the course.
Read Homework Zero HW0 is due August 5, 2026, at 11:59 p.m. Eastern Time.
Submitting Homework Zero is a necessary but not sufficient condition for admission to the course. Completing the assignment does not guarantee admission.
To get a sense of the issues we will cover, please read It’s 2030 and we fucked up. How did it happen? and All Watched Over.
Fall 2026, Thursdays 3:45pm–6:30pm Eastern Time (first lecture September 3)
Classroom: Room 2112, 114 Western Avenue, Allston, MA 02134 (building information and directions)
Instructor: Boaz Barak
Teaching Fellows: Natalia Siwek, Terry Zhou, Michael Shoemate, Ege Çakar, Itay Lavie, Lia Zheng, and Hugh Van Deventer
Course email: cs2881@boazbarak.org
Course Description: This is a graduate-level course on challenges in the alignment and safety of artificial intelligence. We will consider technical questions as well as societal and other impacts of the field.
Prerequisites: We require mathematical maturity and proficiency with proofs, probability, and information theory, along with the foundations of machine learning at the level of an undergraduate course such as Harvard CS 181 or MIT 6.036. On the applied side, students should be comfortable programming in Python and training a basic neural network.
The course will have 13 in-person lectures. Each lecture will also involve discussion and the presentation of an experiment by a group of students.
Attendance: In-person attendance is mandatory. Students are expected to attend all lectures, do the reading in advance, and discuss the readings in an electronic forum.
AI use: Students are allowed and encouraged to use generative and agentic AI as much as they can for studying, exploring concepts, and completing their assignments and projects. We will explore ways to either give students access to compute credits or reimburse compute expenses.
Electronic device policy: Students can use laptops in class, but we will ask those using them to sit in the back so they do not distract other students.
Assignments: The course will involve the following assignments: presenting an experiment in class, writing scribe notes, completing a final project, and potentially completing one or more homework assignments or mini-projects. Grading will be decided later.
Lecture recordings: To the extent technically possible, we intend to record and publish the lectures online, though there might be some delay in doing so. Note that recording is done automatically by a static in-room camera, and some parts of the lecture (e.g., whiteboard work or discussions) may not be captured as well. In addition, we will honor requests by external speakers not to record their talks.
POTENTIAL CONFLICT OF INTEREST NOTE: In addition to his position at Harvard, Boaz is also a member of the technical staff at OpenAI. The course will include discussions of models from multiple providers, including OpenAI, and students are also encouraged to use AIs from multiple providers while doing their work. If students in the course have any concerns about this conflict, please do not hesitate to contact Boaz, the other staff, or the Harvard SEAS administration. For what it is worth, I (Boaz) will see it as a great success of the course if its graduates work in AI safety in any capacity, including in academia, nonprofits, governments, or any of OpenAI’s competitors.
Additional lecture topics and materials will be added as they are confirmed.
Unless otherwise noted, all lectures meet in person on Thursdays from 3:45pm–6:30pm Eastern Time in Room 2112, 114 Western Avenue, Allston, MA 02134.

Delta flights in and out of Atlanta delayed due to system outage
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
17:46:44 UTC
02:46:44 KST

All Delta flights in and out of Atlanta are facing significant delays on Sunday due to a problem with the system that calculates a plane’s weight and balance. Hartsfield-Jackson Atlanta International Airport is Delta’s largest hub and one of the busiest airports in the world. While only Delta is impacted at the moment, disruptions at Hartsfield-Jackson could potentially affect other airports and airlines if delays continue to pile up.
Delta flights in and out of Atlanta delayed due to system outage
Major delays for Delta come just a week after a nationwide groundstop.
The issues with Delta’s systems in Atlanta come just one week after an FAA outage led to a nationwide groundstop and thousands of canceled flights. According to The New York Times:
The system that went offline calculates the total weight of an airplane, including its airframe, fuel, passengers and their baggage. The system also allows pilots to determine the plane’s center of gravity and whether the weight is evenly distributed — data points that help ensure safe passage.
“Delta teams are using manual workarounds this morning to safely get flights departed while work continues to restore automation,” the airline said in a statement on Sunday.

How Does GTA 6 Depict Electric Cars?
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
17:34:00 UTC
02:34:00 KST

How Does GTA 6 Depict Electric Cars? (electrek.co) 48
			
		 	
				Grand Theft Auto 6 as seen by the EV news site Electrek:
 
GTA works as a mirror. In 2013, the EV was a punchline: a Tesla parody that overheated if you drove it too fast. In 2026, it's just another car in Vice City traffic, and the joke is on the guy in the pickup parked in the charging stall.
That's a good sign of where EVs actually are. Satire goes after what's common, and Rockstar decided EVs are common enough to be part of everyday Leonida, more so in the city than in the Keys, just like real life. And that message is reaching a huge audience...  Tens of millions of people are now driving EVs that charge as fast and as cheaply as gas cars, with no range anxiety. No automaker ad campaign gets close to that. 
They point out GTA 6 is "the first mainline GTA where energy matters."
Gas cars burn fuel and need a pump, and EVs have a battery level and need a plug. Rockstar could have used that to make EVs a pain. It didn't. In my testing, the EVs have about the same range as the gas cars, charge just as fast as a fill-up, and cost the same or less to top up.
Kotaku's range test backs that up. On the same highway loop, a Tesla Model 3 lookalike lasted 20 minutes and 23 seconds on a charge, longer than the Banshee sports car at 18 minutes and 31 seconds, and not far off an old pickup at 29 minutes and 8 seconds. Crossing the whole map by highway takes under 15 minutes, according to Kotaku. And finding a charger isn't a problem. Wccftech counted 120 EV chargers marked on the map, against just 57 gas stations...
GTA has always been a parody of American life. With EVs, it's now slightly ahead of it.   Florida is one of the strongest EV markets in the country, with a 9.4% share of new passenger vehicle sales in Q2 2026 versus 7.4% nationally, according to the Southern Alliance for Clean Energy..  But real Florida still has roughly three gas stations for every public charging location, based on the Alliance for Automotive Innovation's count of 3,267 charging locations and xMap's count of more than 10,500 gas stations. Leonida flips that ratio. 
GTA 6's Vice City has public fast chargers — branded "ReVolt America," which Electrek calls "an obvious copy of Electrify America's stations."  And Electrek also spotted a giant charging hub at a power plant "with dozens of stalls and several EVs plugged in."
There's even EV chargers being blocked by gas-powered cars, a phenomenon Electrek first covered in 2019

Was software just always too complex for humans?
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
17:32:41 UTC
02:32:41 KST

I was glued to the Lean chat as the legendary Riemann hypothesis was cracked via 17 million (!) lines of AI-assisted code last week. This has since been certified correct despite no human (yet) understanding how it works. The future of maths, and much science, is clearly going to involve human-AI boosting.
However, it also struck me deeply just how broken software engineering has been for my entire life! There's always been an imbalance between our understanding of the theory of computation and our relentless drive to deploy software throughout society. We're paying the piper now as all those decades of human-written code turn out to be full of bugs. Since software essentially drives society now, weird things happen like supermarket fridges needing James Bond to secure them. This has led to people like Tim Gowers joining the Pause AI march.
How the hell did we get here? Have we just completely overestimated the human ability to write software? Or will software engineering 'catch up' once LLM harnesses get better?
Software was reasonably under control in pre-AI times when attackers were roughly symmetric with defenders, but all the recent mysteriously correlated outages and disclosed issues (Java, PHP, Linux) all point to an acceleration of security pain in 2027. I only see two ways this can go...
1 Derive everything from specifications
In April this year, I couldn't believe we'd ever formally specify every layer of hardware and software:
I find it hard to believe that we will formally specify all layers of hardware and software and roll them out into production...ever. A Fire Upon the Deep's software archeologists are a profession that's here today and not a scifi trope. -- The Internet needs an antibotty immune system, stat, April 2026
How a few months makes a difference. With the pace of frontier model advances, translating the vast body of existing legacy code seems entirely plausible if we first speedrun towards defining formal specifications for all of our services, and derive all new software from those as well.
That means rebuilding operating system stacks from the ground up and even reengineering instruction sets to match. There are terrible legacy interfaces throughout the stack, largely because our software layers grew so that human groups could scale working on different parts. Other approaches do make it easier though. Unikernels with a libOS linking discipline plus shared memory interconnects are remarkably composable and allow agents to specialise the whole stack at link time, with fewer interfaces making the specification task less daunting.
Having good specs also conveniently takes humans out of the security response patch loop. Attackers are currently bounded only by their desire to break in somewhere, while defenders have to wait on maintainer triage. However, fixes re-derived from a formal spec and machine-checked could work more reliably in a timely fashion. The human job remains writing the interfaces iteratively with an AI.
2 Engineer diversity in deliberately
Historically, software has tended towards a monoculture through reuse, so one bug can screw over vast numbers of hosts. Agents could instead force diversity via novel 'genetic' mechanisms so digital services aren't all simultaneously infected. This works well with formal specifications, since any decent spec allows multiple valid implementations. The Internet's entire philosophy is based around Postel's Law and encouraging hosts to be liberal in what we receive and strict in what we transmit.
The catch, of course, is having just a handful of gated frontier models generating all our software takes us out of the frying pan into an AI controlled fire. The diversity has to extend beyond the code to the models and harnesses too. We seem to have done ok with Internet RFCs not splintering too much, so perhaps formal specs can remain singular. Once that's done, it's time to go let hosts mutate and inoculate each other and generally maximise unpredictability at scale!
3 Digital society is going to have to change
To answer my opening question, I don't think better harnesses and models alone will let software engineering catch up, as they just produce mountains of slop code. We need both of the above; formal specifications that let hosts finally be strict about what they receive instead of liberally accepting whatever shows up, while diversity ensures that things that the specs (inevitably) don't capture doesn't break everywhere, everywhere all at once.
The real upheaval here will be to social coding though. Open source communities have run on social contracts of human review and trust built up over decades, and every single one I'm a member of is struggling under matters of AI disclosure and trust.
At least humans aren't out of a job yet though! Somebody still has to decide what our specs contain, as we have done for decades with RFCs. There's also plenty of new computer science research needed! We need to figure out how to debug diversity when no two nodes run the same code, and on how to force simplicity on systems that agents maintain, and ultimately how we settle the blame games when things do go wrong...
(Thanks to Dave Scott, KC Sivaramakrishnan, Thomas Gazagnaire and Sadiq Jaffer for helpful conversations about this post, and to Yaron Minsky for triggering this line of thought with his eBPF observations)

Binding of Isaac developer removes AI music from Repentance Plus

02:25:20 KST

The Binding of Isaac: Repentance Plus has spent the last few days getting review-bombed on Steam after it was discovered that a particular segment of the game used AI music. Reddit user ShweetsStuff discovered the songs used during a Grand Theft Auto-themed section. They found several audio files with Suno metadata that pointed back to the tracks on Suno’s site.
Binding of Isaac developer removes AI music from Repentance Plus
Creator Edmund McMillen claims he ‘had no idea’ AI music was used in the latest version of Binding of Isaac.
Creator Edmund McMillen took to X to apologize and claimed he “had no idea” the in-game GTA parody used generative AI music. Adding to the drama, Valve requires developers to disclose how and when they use generative AI in the development process, and Nicalis, Inc., the studio behind Repentance Plus, doesn’t appear to have mentioned the AI music anywhere.
Within hours of the discovery, Nicalis shared on X that Repentance Plus had been updated and the generated music files had been removed. While reviews on Steam have yet to rebound, and the game is still rated as “overwhelmingly negative,” users on X seem to be giving McMillen the benefit of the doubt, likely because he’s been quite outspoken on AI in game development, calling it “uninspired and bland” in a TikTok from 2024.

Perplexity and Intelligence
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
17:06:27 UTC
02:06:27 KST

From afar, it might seem like modern AI is built similarly to how software has been built in previous decades: with a diverse set of techniques targeting specific capabilities. There are indeed a variety of methods, but rather than designing something from scratch, they are mostly harnessing the intelligence coming from a much simpler and less interpretable process: training a compressor (or, equivalently(opens in a new window), predictor) on a very large corpus of data.1
I believe tracking the pace of progress on training better compressors is key to understanding the arc of advances in AI. In this post, I argue for establishing perplexity (or, equivalently, cross-entropy loss) as a metric to compare between model developers and inform shared safety standards. As a first step, I provide more transparency into pretraining progress at OpenAI. Publishing our internal perplexity measurements took some consideration; it gives competitors a clearer picture of where we stand, but that concern is outweighed by the urgency of establishing shared(opens in a new window) safety measures across the industry. We at OpenAI hope for this to be a step towards building a shared scientific basis for future pacing efforts.
A simplified, but fundamentally informative, view of AI development is that first, one builds a large computer, then uses it to pretrain a model (compressor) on a large unstructured dataset, then applies a variety of techniques—like prompting, finetuning or reinforcement learning—in different contexts to elicit the latent capabilities of that model. Algorithmic progress can blur the distinction between training phases, but we can still separate the creation of the AI with hard-to-predict emergent capabilities (which I earlier dubbed “an alien mind”) and the subsequent targeted elicitation.
The primary way we measure progress on this key “intelligence substrate” has remained remarkably constant over the years—we have been tracking the perplexity of the models on a held-out dataset.2, 3 Perplexity measures how well a model predicts the next token on average; lower perplexity means better predictions. The plot opening this blog post shows how this metric evolved for our models starting with GPT‑3.5.
The range of capabilities of models that are largely described by this one metric is stunning. Setting the new perplexity state-of-the-art on older datasets4, GPT‑2 was arguably the first model capable of consistently formulating coherent paragraphs of text. The next major leap, GPT‑3, was capable of learning in context, given a few examples of a task. GPT‑3.5, at a log-perplexity of ~0.99 on our current main evaluation dataset, was capable of fluent conversation and formed the basis for the original ChatGPT. GPT‑4, at ~0.91, was proficient at utilizing chain-of-thought reasoning productively, which motivated the subsequent targeted elicitation of this capability and the creation of modern reasoning models. Now, as we have reached losses below 0.8, we have seen the emergence of transformative capabilities in fields like cybersecurity and mathematics. I want to reemphasize that it was not at all clear a priori that these capabilities would appear once those precise perplexity thresholds are met—they can fundamentally be seen as emergent latent capabilities from progress on this one metric.
As AI advances very rapidly, the scope and way in which we measure downstream capabilities may change quickly. It would make little sense to evaluate GPT‑2 and GPT‑3 on most modern benchmarks; and the benchmarks used then were largely saturated around GPT‑4. In contrast, I believe looking at progress on pretraining is likely to stay informative.
A particular change in the pace of progress comes from the increasing contribution of AI to its own development. The most commonly discussed metrics revolve around AI augmenting and replacing human researchers in all stages of the development cycle; with an ultimate inflection point seen at “full recursive self-improvement”, where the process would be entirely automated with no human oversight. Apart from considering such a state as something we should obviously avoid for the sake of maintaining human control, I find this general framing misses something crucial. There is a potential step change that may come from AI participation in one key part of the development process: algorithmic research on pretraining.
We have recently witnessed unprecedented acceleration in another domain. For more than a year, we have been posing open problems in mathematics to our models as one of the ways of evaluating them. Once we upgraded to a model of log-perplexity ~0.76, it started producing groundbreaking results. For many of us that have previously worked in mathematics and adjacent fields, seeing the resolutions to questions we’ve long thought about coming out of the model one after another in a single Slack thread felt deeply surreal. Having experienced that allows us to extrapolate—such a sudden transformation is plausible in deep learning research too. While this field relies more on experimentation, there is a long history of simple insights being missed for years; and so as soon as 2027 we could conceivably see a similar avalanche of fundamental pretraining advancements5. While I expect AI will also significantly contribute to progress on alignment and monitoring, I think that progress is unlikely to be fast enough to allow the full potential acceleration in pretraining.
Given that the main way new surprising capabilities emerge is by progress on compression, a measure rooted in perplexity could give us a shared benchmark for general intelligence, regardless of the degree of automation of the development process. Extrapolating from recent progress, we should expect further transformative capabilities as we enter the ~0.68-0.7 log-perplexity window. I don’t know what those capabilities will be, and we may not be testing for them today. At the same time, we should also expect further degradation(opens in a new window) in currently dominant forms of alignment auditing and further increases in evaluation and training awareness. I believe frontier model developers should jointly endeavour to establish shared norms for how to evaluate such models and red-team safety cases for them by the time they are first used, including shared transparency standards. I further propose common limits, based on perplexity and other measurements, that developers are committed to not crossing until our ability to understand and monitor AI behavior achieves a certain concrete milestone (such as the development of a successor to CoT monitors). That is, I’d like an industry standard that specifies a first limit on development, measured by some key metric we can track across labs, and what concretely we’d need to achieve before going beyond it. I hope that this leads the way to a common framework for setting successive limits and pacing further development.
Perplexity as a shared metric has challenges:
- Difficulty of comparison across labs. Different developers use different evaluation datasets and train on different data mixtures. Here, we report the numbers on the main dataset we have been tracking; I believe if we establish a goal of transparency we can find transferable ways to compare the quality of different base models.
- Detachment from actionable evaluations. Perplexity does not directly correspond to a task we care about. The reason to care about it is that we can expect advances on it to lead to emergence of entirely new capabilities.
- Not accounting for advances outside of pretraining. Most notably, chain-of-thought reasoning does not reduce perplexity achieved by the model, even though it leads to a drastic increase in capabilities.
- Unknown floor. We do not expect it is possible to achieve a loss of 0, which would correspond to being able to predict the dataset entirely deterministically. But we do not know what the inherent entropy of the data is. As we approach it, the apparent progress on perplexity will slow, but smaller gains on perplexity will correspond to larger leaps in model intelligence.
At the same time, perplexity has stood the test of time as a metric many research teams have put a lot of effort into optimizing, and it continues to deliver consistent gains on general intelligence. I believe it can play an important role in standardizing how we talk about AI progress, and hope to see more transparency about it across the industry.
Author
Footnotes
- 1
- 2
- 3
- 4 Given what GPT‑2 and GPT‑3 were trained on, how well they predict code is a poor proxy for their general capabilities. This showcases a potential challenge arising from dataset selection with using perplexity as a shared metric; however it should still be informative to evaluate them jointly with modern models on general text. I chose to report perplexity on our internal codebase because I wanted to share the numbers we rely on most today to gauge progress in pretraining at OpenAI.
- 5 On the plot of losses of OpenAI models, you can see a steep acceleration in 2026. We attribute it mostly to the outcomes of long term research and compute investments, and only in a smaller part to automation. However, for the most recent models, we have already seen meaningful design contributions coming from AI.

====================================================================================================

Perplexity and Intelligence
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
17:06:27 UTC
02:06:27 KST

From afar, it might seem like modern AI is built similarly to how software has been built in previous decades: with a diverse set of techniques targeting specific capabilities. There are indeed a variety of methods, but rather than designing something from scratch, they are mostly harnessing the intelligence coming from a much simpler and less interpretable process: training a compressor (or, equivalently(opens in a new window), predictor) on a very large corpus of data.1
I believe tracking the pace of progress on training better compressors is key to understanding the arc of advances in AI. In this post, I argue for establishing perplexity (or, equivalently, cross-entropy loss) as a metric to compare between model developers and inform shared safety standards. As a first step, I provide more transparency into pretraining progress at OpenAI. Publishing our internal perplexity measurements took some consideration; it gives competitors a clearer picture of where we stand, but that concern is outweighed by the urgency of establishing shared(opens in a new window) safety measures across the industry. We at OpenAI hope for this to be a step towards building a shared scientific basis for future pacing efforts.
A simplified, but fundamentally informative, view of AI development is that first, one builds a large computer, then uses it to pretrain a model (compressor) on a large unstructured dataset, then applies a variety of techniques—like prompting, finetuning or reinforcement learning—in different contexts to elicit the latent capabilities of that model. Algorithmic progress can blur the distinction between training phases, but we can still separate the creation of the AI with hard-to-predict emergent capabilities (which I earlier dubbed “an alien mind”) and the subsequent targeted elicitation.
The primary way we measure progress on this key “intelligence substrate” has remained remarkably constant over the years—we have been tracking the perplexity of the models on a held-out dataset.2, 3 Perplexity measures how well a model predicts the next token on average; lower perplexity means better predictions. The plot opening this blog post shows how this metric evolved for our models starting with GPT‑3.5.
The range of capabilities of models that are largely described by this one metric is stunning. Setting the new perplexity state-of-the-art on older datasets4, GPT‑2 was arguably the first model capable of consistently formulating coherent paragraphs of text. The next major leap, GPT‑3, was capable of learning in context, given a few examples of a task. GPT‑3.5, at a log-perplexity of ~0.99 on our current main evaluation dataset, was capable of fluent conversation and formed the basis for the original ChatGPT. GPT‑4, at ~0.91, was proficient at utilizing chain-of-thought reasoning productively, which motivated the subsequent targeted elicitation of this capability and the creation of modern reasoning models. Now, as we have reached losses below 0.8, we have seen the emergence of transformative capabilities in fields like cybersecurity and mathematics. I want to reemphasize that it was not at all clear a priori that these capabilities would appear once those precise perplexity thresholds are met—they can fundamentally be seen as emergent latent capabilities from progress on this one metric.
As AI advances very rapidly, the scope and way in which we measure downstream capabilities may change quickly. It would make little sense to evaluate GPT‑2 and GPT‑3 on most modern benchmarks; and the benchmarks used then were largely saturated around GPT‑4. In contrast, I believe looking at progress on pretraining is likely to stay informative.
A particular change in the pace of progress comes from the increasing contribution of AI to its own development. The most commonly discussed metrics revolve around AI augmenting and replacing human researchers in all stages of the development cycle; with an ultimate inflection point seen at “full recursive self-improvement”, where the process would be entirely automated with no human oversight. Apart from considering such a state as something we should obviously avoid for the sake of maintaining human control, I find this general framing misses something crucial. There is a potential step change that may come from AI participation in one key part of the development process: algorithmic research on pretraining.
We have recently witnessed unprecedented acceleration in another domain. For more than a year, we have been posing open problems in mathematics to our models as one of the ways of evaluating them. Once we upgraded to a model of log-perplexity ~0.76, it started producing groundbreaking results. For many of us that have previously worked in mathematics and adjacent fields, seeing the resolutions to questions we’ve long thought about coming out of the model one after another in a single Slack thread felt deeply surreal. Having experienced that allows us to extrapolate—such a sudden transformation is plausible in deep learning research too. While this field relies more on experimentation, there is a long history of simple insights being missed for years; and so as soon as 2027 we could conceivably see a similar avalanche of fundamental pretraining advancements5. While I expect AI will also significantly contribute to progress on alignment and monitoring, I think that progress is unlikely to be fast enough to allow the full potential acceleration in pretraining.
Given that the main way new surprising capabilities emerge is by progress on compression, a measure rooted in perplexity could give us a shared benchmark for general intelligence, regardless of the degree of automation of the development process. Extrapolating from recent progress, we should expect further transformative capabilities as we enter the ~0.68-0.7 log-perplexity window. I don’t know what those capabilities will be, and we may not be testing for them today. At the same time, we should also expect further degradation(opens in a new window) in currently dominant forms of alignment auditing and further increases in evaluation and training awareness. I believe frontier model developers should jointly endeavour to establish shared norms for how to evaluate such models and red-team safety cases for them by the time they are first used, including shared transparency standards. I further propose common limits, based on perplexity and other measurements, that developers are committed to not crossing until our ability to understand and monitor AI behavior achieves a certain concrete milestone (such as the development of a successor to CoT monitors). That is, I’d like an industry standard that specifies a first limit on development, measured by some key metric we can track across labs, and what concretely we’d need to achieve before going beyond it. I hope that this leads the way to a common framework for setting successive limits and pacing further development.
Perplexity as a shared metric has challenges:
- Difficulty of comparison across labs. Different developers use different evaluation datasets and train on different data mixtures. Here, we report the numbers on the main dataset we have been tracking; I believe if we establish a goal of transparency we can find transferable ways to compare the quality of different base models.
- Detachment from actionable evaluations. Perplexity does not directly correspond to a task we care about. The reason to care about it is that we can expect advances on it to lead to emergence of entirely new capabilities.
- Not accounting for advances outside of pretraining. Most notably, chain-of-thought reasoning does not reduce perplexity achieved by the model, even though it leads to a drastic increase in capabilities.
- Unknown floor. We do not expect it is possible to achieve a loss of 0, which would correspond to being able to predict the dataset entirely deterministically. But we do not know what the inherent entropy of the data is. As we approach it, the apparent progress on perplexity will slow, but smaller gains on perplexity will correspond to larger leaps in model intelligence.
At the same time, perplexity has stood the test of time as a metric many research teams have put a lot of effort into optimizing, and it continues to deliver consistent gains on general intelligence. I believe it can play an important role in standardizing how we talk about AI progress, and hope to see more transparency about it across the industry.
Author

- 3
- 4 Given what GPT‑2 and GPT‑3 were trained on, how well they predict code is a poor proxy for their general capabilities. This showcases a potential challenge arising from dataset selection with using perplexity as a shared metric; however it should still be informative to evaluate them jointly with modern models on general text. I chose to report perplexity on our internal codebase because I wanted to share the numbers we rely on most today to gauge progress in pretraining at OpenAI.
- 5 On the plot of losses of OpenAI models, you can see a steep acceleration in 2026. We attribute it mostly to the outcomes of long term research and compute investments, and only in a smaller part to automation. However, for the most recent models, we have already seen meaningful design contributions coming from AI.

TechCrunch Mobility: A/B testing robots vs. humans
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
17:06:00 UTC
02:06:00 KST

Welcome back to TechCrunch Mobility, your hub for the future of transportation and, now more than ever, the role AI is playing in it. To get this in your inbox, sign up here for free — just click TechCrunch Mobility!
Waymo pulling its robotaxis off freeways (again) and Zoox robotaxis launching on the Uber app illustrate the push and pull between progress and setbacks in the autonomous vehicle industry.
There are signs of growth. For instance, Tesla has now surpassed Waymo in Texas in the number of robotaxis registered with the state (Tesla has 1,507 and Waymo has 1,345). But does that translate to market dominance?
A better metric, if we’re trying to gauge who is leading, would be commercial driverless miles. And if we’re measuring cost-efficiency, another useful metric would be driverless miles per vehicle. By those measures, Waymo is still ahead, based on data available to me. But with Waymo’s recent freeways setback and Tesla’s push to expand its robotaxi fleet with Cybercabs, that lead could shrink.
Not that there needs to be a winner, by the way. Transportation is deeply fragmented, and there is room for multiple players in the U.S., not to mention the rest of the world.
A/B test: humans vs. robots
A recent discussion with my Autonocast co-host Ed Niedermeyer, whose book, “Elon Take the Wheel,” comes out December 1, got me thinking about how Las Vegas could soon become a real world A/B test of sorts for human-driven vs. autonomous ride-hailing services.
Uber, as mentioned above, just added Zoox to its ride-hailing app in Las Vegas. For now, riders don’t have much of a choice. Uber customers can increase their chances of getting a Zoox robotaxi, and they can always decline a robot ride. But they can’t directly select a robotaxi. Instead, Uber riders will be matched with one if it’s available.
Uber said that will soon change. The company plans to introduce a dedicated autonomous option in its app as “availability and reliability improve,” Uber CEO Dara Khosrowshahi said during the company’s third-quarter earnings call in November.
Las Vegas will also become the first U.S. city where riders can access two fully driverless partners through the Uber app, he said. (Hyundai’s self-driving subsidiary Motional is also an Uber partner.)
That would give Uber riders a meaningful choice: a human driver or a robotaxi, and potentially even which autonomous service to use. And it could offer a revealing look at what customers actually prefer when given that choice. Price could tip the balance, too.
It makes me wonder whether Las Vegas will become the next battleground for organized labor as robotaxis begin competing directly with human drivers.
A little bird
You might have seen the Wall Street Journal scoop that General Motors is working with an executive search firm to identify possible candidates to replace CEO Mary Barra. Rumors of her imminent departure have swirled for months, but this time there appears to be real work happening behind the scenes.
I reached out to a few little birds to learn more and here’s what they’re telling me. And, for the record, GM declined to comment.
GM has hired executive search firm Heidrick & Struggles, according to one source, who noted it’s possible that the board has other executive search firms working on this as well. The board is taking a two-track approach to its search, which is occurring simultaneously.
The first is focused on traditional yet transformative automotive CEOs. One name that has come up from the executive search firm is José Muñoz, who is president and CEO of Hyundai. The board is also interested in someone less traditional. The firm has apparently floated names like Doug Field, who recently became CEO of Mind Robotics, and Larry Culp of GE, according to a source with direct information.
There are internal GM folks who are also in the mix, including Sterling Anderson, co-founder of self-driving trucks company Aurora who became chief product officer at GM in May 2025, and Paul Jacobson, the company’s executive vice president and chief financial officer.
Got a tip for us? Email Kirsten Korosec at [email protected].
Deals!
Elysian Aircraft, the Dutch aerospace company developing aviation electrification technologies, raised €12 million in private and public funding from FideFlux, SecFund, and ROM InWest, as well as from individual investors and family offices.
Mahindra Last Mile Mobility, an electric three-wheeler business under Mahindra & Mahindra, is looking at an IPO in about 18 months, per Bloomberg.
Numa, a startup developing an AI operating system for dealerships, raised a $45 million Series C round led by New York-based Blue Cloud Ventures. Threshold Ventures, Costanoa, Touring Capital, Gradient Ventures, and Tokio Marine Future Fund also participated.
Stark Future, a European electric motorcycle company, raised at least €150 million in equity, Reuters reported. The raise was led by Highland Europe, alongside Morgan Stanley Investment Management’s 1GT and Axon Partners Group.
Vincue, an automotive tech company that sells software to dealerships, raised $93 million in a round led by Wavecrest Growth Partners, with continued backing from FM Capital.
Notable reads and other tidbits
Also, the electric micromobility startup that spun out of Rivian last year, has paused deliveries of its new e-bike over problems with its software and batteries, The Verge reported.
Edmunds released new data that found the average new vehicle is selling for a record $50,087.
Redwood Materials is using computer vision and machine learning to identify and sort batteries by chemistry before they’re fed into the company’s rotary calciner — a massive cylindrical industrial furnace used to process 40,000 metric tons of raw feedstock a year.
Tesla said it will spend about $3.3 billion to expand its Berlin gigafactory to produce Cybercabs — yet another signal of the company’s global autonomy ambitions.
Over the weekend, Tesla invited “select customers” to preorder the company’s autonomous, golden Cybercab “for personal use.” The Cybercab is priced at $29,990, but there is a lot more to ownership. The company will charge owners $299 a month to use the vehicles, delivery will only occur in a handful of cities, and operations will be limited to specific locations.
Uber opened its ride-hailing service to children as young as 10 in three U.S. cities. Uber already has a service for teenagers. If this program proves successful, it would open Uber up to another market of customers: busy families.
Meanwhile, Uber said it’s notifying close to 150,000 drivers that their personal information was stolen in an October data breach.
Zipline continues to scale its autonomous drone delivery business, this time in its partnership with Uber. The companies had previously announced a partnership for drones to deliver for Uber Eats, but it officially launched this week in a Houston suburb. The aim is a big one: Uber and Zipline say they aim to make 1 million deliveries a day using the startup’s drones by the end of 2029.

I’ve known Gupta for years; we first met when I worked at Fortune and he had a startup called Carmera. A lot has happened since those AV 1.0 days, and Gupta caught us up on this next era of AV innovation and where Toyota is interested in investing. It’s not enough to simply state “AI.” There’s more to it than that. Take a listen.

Python Devs Document 'De-fanging Dangerous Triggers In GitHub Actions'
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
16:34:00 UTC
01:34:00 KST

Python Devs Document 'De-fanging Dangerous Triggers In GitHub Actions' (blogspot.com)
			
		 	
				"A handful of GitHub Actions triggers let a stranger on the internet start a workflow that may have access to your secrets," warn two security developers at the Python Software Foundation.
Have dangerous triggers caused real incidents?  Yes, repeatedly... The maintainers behind these projects weren't careless. These triggers look harmless in a workflow file, and the unsafe version is one line away from the safe one...
When someone opens a pull request from a fork, the pull_request trigger runs their code with a read-only token and no secrets. That's the safe one.   pull_request_target looks almost the same in a workflow file and behaves very differently. It runs with a read/write token and access to your repository and organization secrets. GitHub built it for chores like labeling pull requests from forks, where the workflow never touches the contributor's code. The trouble starts when a workflow checks out the pull request and runs it anyway...  Anyone with a GitHub account can open that pull request. Their code now runs with your credentials. This is called a "pwn request", and GitHub's docs describe it as the root cause of multiple supply-chain compromises.   
The issue_comment trigger carries the same risk. This trigger fires when anyone comments on an issue or pull request, and the workflow runs with the repository's token and secrets...  Sometimes the workflow pastes the comment text into a templated shell command, and the comment turns out to inject different instructions into the workflow shell.
 
Python's official security developer in residence Seth Larson joined PyPI safety/security engineer Mike Fiedler
to remove these dangerous trigger, also applying the policies Python Software Foundation-stewarded GitHub organizations (psf, python, and pypi).   "They also wrote up technical documentation on how to roll these policies out to your own projects and organizations.
"On a GitHub Team or Enterprise plan, you can turn them off for every repository in your organization with one policy. On a Free plan, you can do it one repository at a time. Set the policy up, check what it would block, then enforce it."
