Sheherazaad’s Khudgarzi is gorgeous, ‘genre-chaotic’ South Asian folk
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
16:33:29 UTC
01:33:29 KST

Sheherazaad started making ripples in the music world a few years ago with her EP Qasr, which was produced by Grammy Award winner Arooj Aftab. Now she’s back with Khudgarzi, her reverb-drenched folk-noir debut LP that’s primarily sung in Urdu.
Sheherazaad’s Khudgarzi is gorgeous, ‘genre-chaotic’ South Asian folk
The debut LP from Sheherazaad blends traditional Indian instruments with Western electronic flourishes and feminist narratives.
Sheherazaad’s Khudgarzi is gorgeous, ‘genre-chaotic’ South Asian folk
The debut LP from Sheherazaad blends traditional Indian instruments with Western electronic flourishes and feminist narratives.
The instrumentation on Khudgarzi draws on traditional South Asian music, including Carnatic and Hindustani styles, but also dabbles in Arabic folk traditions, as well as modern Western electronic music. Trip-hop flourishes and Spaghetti Western guitar brush up against tanbur and ghatam. Sheherazaad’s stunning voice sits atop dripping percussion and seasick drones singing about the plight of “The Selfish Woman.”
For Sheherazaad, the character of the selfish woman isn’t the villain, it’s a charge leveled at women who didn’t conform to societal norms, “eccentric women whose desire for agency was often met with an awful end.” Even if you can’t understand most of the lyrics, the atmosphere tells the story clearly enough. From the barely simmering anger of “Registaan” to the bleak repetition of “Qatil” and the fantastical yearning of “Zeher,” Khudgarzi is clearly the work of a woman exploring what it means to be a woman, the lasting legacy of colonialism, and the perpetual threats of a patriarchal society.
Khudgarzi is alternately a funeral dirge and a celebration of femininity. Sheherazaad owes a lot of the album’s ability to navigate these various themes and moods to her impressive production work, top-notch mixing by J. Valleau, and the cast of over 30 “genre-chaotic” virtuosic musicians including Ria Modak, Sarathy Korwar, Jay Chakravorty, Shunya, and Qasim Naqvi. The instrumentation never distracts, though. It’s not showy, and all emulsifies around Sheherazaad’s vocals in a shawl that feels equal parts comforting and oppressive.
Khudgarzi is available now on Bandcamp and most major streaming platforms, including YouTube Music, Spotify, Apple Music, Deezer, and Qobuz.

C++26 is done (again): November 2026 ISO C++ meeting trip report
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
15:59:59 UTC
00:59:59 KST

tl;dr: A few highlights
- C++26 technical work, originally completed in March, was reopened and is now recompleted: The anticipated plan is to reissue the updated C++26 document for a second approval ballot.
- Most of the six-day meeting was devoted to addressing a second set of international comments for C++26, primarily on the contracts language feature.
- Adopted this week for draft C++29: break label; continue label;, std::ranges::any_view, views::slice, and more…
Overview
Today, the ISO C++ committee completed technical work on C++26 for a second time in beautiful Búzios, Rio de Janeiro, Brazil (hybrid online via Zoom).
As I reported in March, the ISO C++ committee completed technical work on C++26 at our March meeting; following that, we held the first meeting of C++29 in June. Since then, the approval (Draft International Standard, or DIS) ballot for C++26 was held but, for the first time, did not pass unanimously and was sent back to the ISO C++ committee with additional international comments to address, mostly about the contracts feature (pre, post, and contract_assert). So this week we addressed the additional comments on C++26, including by adopting an extension to contracts and providing a detailed rationale and timeline to inform national bodies of the history and process by which contracts were developed, concerns heard repeatedly, and decisions made. The committee is now producing an updated final document that is anticipated to be sent out for its second international approval (DIS) ballot and final editorial work, anticipated to be published in the near future by ISO.
This meeting was hosted by C++ Alliance. Thank you to everyone involved and especially to Matheus Izvekov, who took the lead in making the arrangements! Our hosts arranged for high-quality facilities for our six-day meeting from Monday through Saturday. We had about 220 attendees, split about 35%/65% in-person/remote-Zoom, formally representing 27 nations. (The higher-than-usual percentage of remote participation was partly because of the U.S. aviation ground stop that disrupted air travel just as experts were trying to fly to the ISO C++ meeting.) At each meeting we have new guest attendees, and this time there were 13 new guests, mostly in person, in addition to new attendees who are official national body representatives. To all of them, once again welcome!
The committee currently has 22 active subgroups, but this time fewer of them met throughout the week than usual as we focused on the additional C++26 comments.
C++26, take 2
The most controversial feature in C++26 is contracts: pre, post, and contract_assert. The committee already had strong consensus, and as of the end of this meeting has still stronger consensus, on including the feature in C++26, but we spent most of this meeting rehearing contracts concerns and working hard again to address them.
Here is some history (much of this repeated from my March trip report): In February 2025, when we took the plenary poll to adopt contracts in the C++26 working draft (“merge it to trunk”), the vote was:
87.7% in favor:opposed — 100 in favor, 14 opposed, and 12 abstaining
After that, the concerns were all deeply rediscussed for the last four meetings of the original C++26 cycle thanks to thoughtful and high-quality technical papers explaining the objections; all of those papers continued to be fully heard, often for multiple days and at many telecons between meetings, with some contracts fixes adopted.
At our meeting in March 2026, when we took the plenary poll to finalize and ship the C++26 standard, the vote was non-unanimous primarily because of the sustained concerns about contracts:
90.5% in favor:opposed — 114 in favor, 12 opposed, and 3 abstaining
As I reported in March: After extensive scrutiny, the ISO C++ committee still wanted C++26 with contracts, and so contracts stayed in C++26 for the ISO DIS ballot.
Our past ISO C++ DIS approval ballots have always been unanimous. No national bodies (NBs) objected to balloting this C++26 DIS document that included contracts. However, during the ISO DIS process, additional late concerns were raised about contracts, including from five NBs that raised contracts concerns for the first time and voted Disapprove on C++26 because of them (see also my paper P4454R0 section 2.3). The result, which surprised nearly everyone, was that the first DIS was not approved.
Therefore we spent most of the week in Búzios rehearing the NB comments on the DIS, which were mostly about contracts. One requested extension to contracts was adopted in response to NB comments (see next section). Other suggested changes were not adopted, notably the request by multiple comments to remove contracts from C++26; the committee discussed, revoted, and reaffirmed it wants to retain contracts in C++26.
This week, the vote to reship the C++26 standard with the changes to C++26 adopted at this meeting, including an additional extension to contracts in response to the international comments and retaining contracts as part of C++26, was:
92.5% in favor:opposed — 111 in favor, 9 opposed, and 8 abstaining
Thank you again to everyone in the committee who has been working hard together to discuss and address these concerns, including all week long again this week!
Changes to C++26 adopted this week (and for draft C++29)
For C++26, we adopted four main papers and fixed some editorial issues. All of these changes were also adopted for draft C++29, which keeps the standards work in sync rather than forking.
The primary change to contracts was to adopt P4451R2 “Early Adoption of an Always-Enforced Label” by Joshua Berne (in a similar spirit to Romania’s paper P3911R2 “Make Contracts Reliably Non-Ignorable”). Thanks, Romania and Josh!
This paper addresses what several NBs told the committee was their primary request for contracts: It is a pure extension that gives programmers a way to express in source code that a specific contract is always enforced, guaranteeing that “program execution will not continue past the contract assertion if it is violated, regardless of the semantics of other contract assertions” that various parts of the program may be built with. This is forward-compatible with proposals like P3400R5 being considered for C++29.
From the paper:
#include <contracts>
int f(int x)
    pre<std::contracts::controls::enforced>(x > 0)
    post<std::contracts::controls::enforced>(r : r > 0)
{
    contract_assert<std::contracts::controls::enforced>(x > 0);
    return x;
}
// Because the label is found by ordinary name lookup,
// a namespace alias or a using-directive works as well:
namespace scc = std::contracts::controls;
int h(int x)
    pre<scc::enforced>(x > 0)
    post<scc::enforced>(r : r > 0)
{
    contract_assert<scc::enforced>(x > 0);
    return x;
}
using namespace std::contracts::controls;
int g(int x)
    pre<enforced>(x > 0)
    post<enforced>(r : r > 0)
{
    contract_assert<enforced>(x > 0);
    return x;
}
We also adopted the following for C++26:
P4466R2 “Selectively transparent using-declarators (response to FI-067)” by Dan Katz and Tomasz Kamiński addresses a ballot comment to make reflection work more consistently with modules (especially module std), while leaving room for intended fuller support for using-alias reflection in C++29. Thank you, Dan and Tomasz!
P4453R1 “Memory allocation with senders/receivers; a response to IE-133” by Dietmar Kühl and Lucian Radu Teodorescu addresses a ballot comment to offer more flexible control over memory allocation with std::execution. Much appreciated, Dietmar and Lucian!
P3861R1 “Pragmatic approach to standard structural types” by Corentin Jabot makes reflection more useful. Thanks, Corentin! This paper allows more use of string_view, span, and tuple, including to use them as template parameters and to create type-safe strings at compile time that can be used at execution time.
Adopted for C++29: Core language changes/features
We actually also did some work on C++29! Here are some highlights.
These links are to the most recent public version of each paper. If a paper was tweaked at the meeting before being approved, the link tracks and will automatically find the updated version as soon as it’s uploaded to the public site.
Several of the adopted language and library additions are already supported today in major C++ implementations. “Adopted for C++29” doesn’t mean “have to wait until 2029 or later to start seeing some support in real compilers / standard libraries.”
In addition to fixing a list of issues, we adopted five papers for the C++29 core language, including the following…
P0085R5 “Oo… adding a coherent character sequence to begin octal-literals” by Jolly Chen, Axel Naumann, Michael Jonker (all of CERN) gets the award for “most patient paper adopted at this meeting” — thanks, Jolly, Axel, and Michael! Its initial revision was published in 2015, and this week we adopted it: Finally, portable C++ will support “0o” (zero, lowercase-O) and “0O” (zero, uppercase-O) as a way to introduce octal literals, and the old prefix “0” is deprecated for that purpose.
P3568R4 “break label; and continue label;” by Jan Schultke adds labeled break and continue to draft standard C++. Thanks, Jan! A loop can now be preceded by a “label:” (or more than one) that names it, and can be a target for break and continue to say which of a set of nested loops is intended. Here is an example from the updated standard wording itself (lightly edited):
a: b: while (/* ... */) {
  a: a: c: for (/* ... */) {
    break;          // OK, terminates enclosing for loop
    break a;        // OK, same
    break b;        // OK, terminates enclosing while loop
    y: { break y; } // error: y is not a breakable statement
  }
  break c;          // error: c is not an enclosing statement
}
We also added several other language extensions and improvements.
Adopted for C++29: Standard library changes/features
In addition to fixing a list of issues, we adopted seven papers for the C++29 standard library, including the following…
P3411R7 “any_view” by Hui Xie, Louis Dionne, S. Levent Yilmaz, and Patrick Roberts adds the type-erased std::ranges::any_view to the standard library. Thank you, authors! The paper’s abstract summarizes it well: “That type-erased view allows customizing the traversal category of the view, its value type and a few other properties. For example:”
class MyClass {
    std::unordered_map<Key, Widget> widgets_;
public:
    std::ranges::any_view<Widget> getWidgets();
};
std::ranges::any_view<Widget> MyClass::getWidgets() {
    return widgets_ | std::views::values
                    | std::views::filter(myFilter);
}
P3216R6 “views::slice” by Hewill Kang adds the views::slice adaptor to the draft C++29 ranges library. Thanks, Hewill! This provides a standard way to extract a contiguous subrange from a sequence by specifying a start index and an end index. It is also the first adaptor that takes two parameters (start and end).
P4189R0 “get()ing the pointer from optional” by Nevin Liber provides a portable way to get a pointer to the contained object of a std::optional by adding the .get_ptr() member function. Thank you, Nevin!
We also added several other library extensions and improvements.
Wrapping up
Thank you again to the about 220 experts who attended in person and online at this week’s meeting, and the many more who participate in standardization through their national bodies!
But we’re not slowing down… we’ll continue to have subgroup Zoom meetings, and then our next full meeting will be in Istanbul, Türkiye, in March, where we’ll continue adding features to C++29. I look forward to seeing many of you there.
Thank you to everyone reading this for your interest and support for C++ and its standardization.

====================================================================================================

Norway to extend proposed under-16 social media ban to games and messaging apps
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
15:54:24 UTC
00:54:24 KST

Online games and messaging services were initially expected to receive broad exemptions, but the government has now decided to assess platforms based on their potential risks to children.
Norway is set to introduce a social media ban for children under 16, with the government announcing on Monday that it is finalising legislation to be submitted to parliament by the end of the year.
The proposed restrictions will also cover potentially harmful online games and messaging services, beyond conventional social media platforms, the government said in a statement.
TikTok, Snapchat and Roblox, a popular online gaming platform where users can create and play games while interacting with others, would likely fall under the proposed law.
"Children's safety must come first," said Lene Vågslid, Norway's minister for children and families, adding that the legislation was nearly complete.
The restrictions aim to protect children from harmful content, addictive features, illegal advertising and unwanted contact from strangers.
Online games and messaging services were initially expected to receive broad exemptions, but the government has now decided to assess platforms based on their potential risks to children.
This means services considered harmless, such as messaging apps used for school communication, could remain accessible.
Vågslid said the government was also safeguarding children's "right to participate," although Monday's announcement did not explain how this would be ensured under the proposed restrictions.
Technology companies would be responsible for verifying users' ages, with systems required to comply with European data protection rules.
Norway's centre-left Labour government published a draft bill in 2025 proposing a minimum social media age of 15 before revising the threshold to 16 in April this year. The legislation has since undergone public consultation in Norway and the European Economic Area (EEA).
Social media restrictions in Europe
Norway joins a growing number of European countries moving to restrict children's access to social media amid concerns about online safety and addiction.
Denmark has adopted similar restrictions, while Spain is pursuing a ban for pupils under 16.
In September, France attempted to ban social media for children under 15, but its Constitutional Council struck it down over freedom of expression concerns.
At the European Union level, the European Commission proposed the EU Kids Act in September, which would also introduce age-based restrictions on social media, online games and other digital services.

====================================================================================================

I joined the NTP Pool, but not with my Stratum 1 Pi
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
15:35:24 UTC
00:35:24 KST

I joined the NTP Pool, but not with my Stratum 1 Pi
Before I get too deep into the time-nuts rabbit hole, I figured I should share a little bit of the good time I now have in my studio, with everyone else.
Thus, I've finally joined the NTP Pool, offering one public Stratum 2 NTP time server (ntp-01.jeffgeerling.com), which gets its time from my Stratum 1 time server (time.jeffgeerling.com) in the studio.
My Time Pi is connected to an outside GNSS antenna through a TimeHAT v6, which I paired with a high quality timing-grade GNSS module, the u-blox ZED-F9T.
I posted a video about the setup today on YouTube:
I'd encourage anyone else interested in network time to contribute to the NTP Pool. However, there are some things you should know before contributing your own time services:
Time Commitment
You don't need to go all out with a timing-grade GNSS module like I'm using. Heck, you don't even need GNSS-backed time at all!
The main requirements for hosting a node in the global NTP pool are:
- Keeping the system online (servers are only made active in the pool if they're online and serving good time consistently for a long period)
- Maintaining a good time—which can mean Stratum 1 GNSS service, but could also mean pulling time from a few other reliable time servers, and running as Stratum 2 or 3
- A stable IP address (IPv4 and/or IPv6), and a long-term commitment
Just 'testing the waters' in the Pool wastes resources—when you bring a new server online, it will take at least a few hours just to spin up all the tests to verify the server's providing a good time.
If you just want to operate your own time server, that's fine; the NTP Pool is a shared global resource, so joining it is a little more of a commitment.
Two other important caveats:
- Your NTP server's IP address will be public
- NTP traffic will slowly decay, and some misconfigured systems could take months or even years before they'll stop sending NTP traffic at your IP
The first caveat matters a lot to me: back when I exposed my home IP address trying to self-host this website on a cluster of Raspberry Pis, some doofus decided to DDoS my website—and was persistently doing so for the better part of a month.
I had to cycle my home IP, then put my site behind Cloudflare, just to protect my poor Pis (and eventually the VPS I now host it on).
Secure and Measurable Time
For those reasons, I chose to have two layers to my public time services:
- Stratum 1: I have my Time Pi running in the studio, running a WireGuard tunnel to:
- Stratum 2: A VPS I host with a cloud provider in St. Louis, to keep the latency down (ideally with fewer hops across the Internet, which leads to better time sync via NTP)
I include the Stratum 2 time server in my Stratum 1 time server's chrony config as a noselect source, so I can easily compare the Internet delay between the two servers, outside the WireGuard tunnel:
server ntp-01.jeffgeerling.com noselect
This provides me with the following graph:
I've been tracking my time in comparison to an NTP time server hosted at WWV in Ft. Collins, CO (pictured below), one of NIST's NTP servers in Boulder, CO, and Cloudflare.
For typical NTP configurations, you shouldn't single out time servers like this; I'm only doing it for long-term measurement (and I only poll the servers every 1024 seconds). Most systems should use pool addresses, as documented here.
The line for Cloudflare steps up and down sometimes, most likely due to BGP routing: as routes and servers change, packets between St. Louis and time.cloudflare.com can take very different paths there and back.
Note: You might be looking for info on NTS, or Network Time Security—I haven't explored it much, so expect more on that at a later time. Currently the NTP Pool doesn't support NTS, though some have experimented with it. I'm okay with basic NTP and would recommend anyone with critical timing needs to use other time sources as well (don't only rely on NTP!).
Chrony and GPS time tweaks
My Time Pi is not only serving as a Stratum 1 timing reference for my public NTP server, it's also my primary reference for local time in the studio.
The TimeHAT on top does most of the heavy lifting, with its built-in Intel i226 NIC. The i226 includes hardware timestamping, essential for accurate PTP timing on the LAN. To deliver the most accurate time possible, I installed a timing-grade GNSS receiver (u-blox ZED-F9T, built to the M.2 NGFF Sync Module specification.
The entire configuration (for both servers) is managed by Ansible in my time-pi project.
Using the i226, I'm able to discipline the NIC's hardware clock (called the 'PHC', or 'PTP Hardware Clock') in hardware, directly from the GPS module's PPS (Pulse Per Second) pin. Using linuxptp's ts2phc tool, I can discipline the PHC to within tens-of-ns of UTC, and distribute that time to other PTP-aware computers on the LAN.
The time on the PHC is shared to my Pi's system clock using refclock PHC inside Chrony, instead of phc2sys, because I was experiencing a weird 200ms jump every so often. Chrony then disciplines the system clock, but this can only be accurate to about a microsecond, because the pulse goes through the PCIe bus, which adds latency I can't measure.
I'm actually trying to make this latency more measurable, since GPIO is routed through the RP1 chip deterministically. See ahmadexp's rp1-ptp-pps utility PR. PTM could reduce the error between the NIC's PHC and the system clock to a few ns, but the BCM2712 SoC on the Pi doesn't support it.
One other quirk I ran into was Debian's default sourcedir /run/chrony-dhcp directive, which was picking up another NTP server via DHCP, causing chrony to stampede my extremely accurate GNSS-derived time from phc2sys with a far-less-accurate time from a public NTP server! (This was another motivation behind moving clock steering to chrony instead of phc2sys.)
Time for an Outage
I'm hosting my public Stratum 2 server with Oxtrys, a VPS provider I'd never used before—and one thing I was nervous about was how they'd react to a VPS handling only UDP packets like this.
Some automated firewalls shut down public NTP servers because NTP traffic may look like abuse. I wondered if that was the case late on the night of November 16, because the server dropped offline while I was writing this blog post:
But looking at the logs further, it seems an unattended upgrade installed a new Debian kernel, then rebooted the server, and the new kernel caused problems. For now the server still runs automatic upgrades, but leaves reboots to me.
Oxtrys' live chat support has been responsive enough, though their account dashboard is broken until November 22, so it doesn't list my server's out-of-band management options.
As you can see in the graph above, the NTP Pool monitors have slowly brought the server's score back up past '10', at which point it can serve time in the pool again. It takes maybe 6–12 hours to recover if the server's been down long enough.
Conclusion
I've certainly learned a lot at every stage of this journey: NTP is loaded with utility, and is currently an essential piece of global timing infrastructure. I think I know about 1% (probably less) of what there is to know about hosting a public NTP server, and hopefully I can share more as I increase that to a few more percentage points!
I'm thankful to all the other NTP Pool operators, as well as all the timelords who built and manage massive NTP endpoints like time.apple.com, time.cloudflare.com, etc.

====================================================================================================

Ask Slashdot: Should an Indie Game Developer Outsource Their Art? 35

 I can draw basic stuff, but nothing close to what I had in mind when I started this project. Is it even worth outsourcing 2D character and environment art at this stage, or should I just push through and do it myself? 
He's "looking for advice from people who've actually dealt with this" to share their stories in the comments.  And Slashdot reader AlexKrav says "Outsourcing saved my project honestly... Quality held from first asset to last. If your vision is clear but the execution is not there yet, handing it off is the right call."  Someone on a thread had suggested game art studios specializing in 3D and 2D game art, and he'd discovered there were lots of choices — 2D Game Art service, VSQUAD Studio.
I've also successfully hired digital artists through freelancing platforms likes Upwork.  Any Slashdot readers want to share their own experiences in hiring artists?  Leave your stories and opinions in the comments.
Should an indie game developer outsource their art?

Fascism and Monopoly
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
15:25:29 UTC
00:25:29 KST

Home > Journals > Michigan Law Review > MLR > Volume 118 > Issue 7 (2020)
Abstract
The recent revival of political interest in antitrust has resurfaced a longstanding debate about the role of industrial concentration and monopoly in enabling Hitler’s rise to power and the Third Reich’s wars of aggression. Proponents of stronger antitrust enforcement argue that monopolies and cartels brought the Nazis to power and warn that rising concentration in the American economy could similarly threaten democracy. Skeptics demur, observing that German big business largely opposed Hitler during the crucial years of his ascent. Drawing on business histories and archival material from the U.S. Office of Military Government’s Decartelization Branch, this Article assesses the historical record on the role of industrial concentration in facilitating Nazism. It finds compelling evidence that, while German big business principally did not support Hitler before he won the chancellorship in 1933, the extreme concentration of market power during the Weimar period enabled Hitler to seize and consolidate totalitarian power through a variety of mechanisms. Hence, the German experience with Nazism lends support to the idea that extreme concentration of economic power enables extreme concentration of political power. However, most of the conduct that created the radical economic concentration of the Weimar period would be unlawful under contemporary antitrust principles, which casts doubt on claims that a significant shift in antitrust enforcement is necessary to forestall antidemocratic forces.
Recommended Citation
    Daniel A. Crane,
    Fascism and Monopoly,
    118
    Mich. L. Rev.
    1315
        (2020).
    
Available at:
        https://repository.law.umich.edu/mlr/vol118/iss7/2
Included in
Antitrust and Trade Regulation Commons, Comparative and Foreign Law Commons, Legal History Commons

A better internet for everyone is possible. Today
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
15:24:32 UTC
00:24:32 KST

The Vision
A shared, standardised communication protocol: it may sound technically complicated, but it is as simple as it is brilliant, and the technical blueprint has long been familiar to all of us: email. Whether your address is hosted by Proton, Posteo, Gmail or GMX, your digital letters reach their destination. This is the idea we build on.
The year is 2028. Thanks to smart investment and political foresight, the Open Social Web has achieved its breakthrough. More than 100 million people connect, communicate and experience the world of open social networks. No platform economy à la Meta, ByteDance, Alphabet and co., no stressful digital moves and no toxic design. It works just like email: whether on Mastodon, Eurosky’s Mu or Streamplace, you have one account, your own posts and content, your own choices, and the freedom to connect with 100 million users worldwide.
A critical mass of users in Europe has turned its back on the platform monopolies and created a free market built on interoperable infrastructure. Today, users decide for themselves which content, which algorithms and which applications they want to use. Closed platforms have become open networks. On the ActivityPub and AT Protocol protocols (as well as Matrix for direct messaging), an ecosystem of non-commercial, public service and commercial offerings has emerged. They enrich and complement public-interest projects such as Mastodon and Eurosky, and they communicate with one another through shared interfaces.
This vision describes the Open Social Web (OSW): a decentralised, public-interest infrastructure for digital communication, based on open standards and interoperable protocols.
The Challenges
The idea of an Open Social Web is not new. The Fediverse has been in development since 2008 and is home to diverse, vibrant communities that build individual applications and run instances. While its focus has so far been on Europe, North America and Japan, there are now more than 100 applications, such as Mastodon, Loops and PeerTube, and more than 42,000 active instances worldwide. Even so, the number of active users, around 13 million, is growing only slowly. The ATmosphere community has been developing since 2022, primarily in North America but increasingly in Europe and beyond. It currently has around 43 million registered accounts and dozens of implementations and applications, such as Eurosky’s Mu, Gander and Sill.
For many reasons, both protocol-based social networks are attractive alternatives to the platform economy dominated by Meta, ByteDance and Alphabet. So why have people been so hesitant to switch?
We have identified five key challenges: infrastructure, governance, funding, appeal and public mandate. Below, we set out how we can overcome them and make the Open Social Web Alliance a shared success.
The Path
An ecosystem like this does not emerge on its own. Our roadmap shows how the Open Social Web could reach a relevant scale within just a few months. It takes political will, smart regulation, sufficient funding and a broad coalition of actors willing to share responsibility. The task is big. But the levers are there.
As a first step, we are founding the Open Social Web Alliance: an open alliance of stakeholders from civil society, research, business, media, culture and the public sector who are committed to the shared governance of the OSW. It brings together a wide range of existing communities and initiatives and is to be supported in future by a publicly funded secretariat. Its central goal: building and operating the OSW’s core infrastructure (see Section 2.1, Infrastructure).
Our roadmap envisions that the German federal government will explicitly support the Alliance, for instance by acting as patron of an Open Social Web Summit in autumn 2026, to which everyone with an interest in the OSW would be invited.
At the summit, the Alliance would adopt its charter, setting out how it admits new members and how it intends to implement its governance going forward (see Section 2.2, Governance). By spring 2027, public funding for the infrastructure would be in place, along with a first core funding round of public and private capital for innovation and applications (see Section 2.3, Funding). The Alliance would publish its first Social Web Health Report, featuring findings from user surveys, legal, technical and academic analyses, and clear recommendations for further development (see Section 2.4, Appeal). In parallel, the federal and state governments would ensure that the legal basis for cooperation between public service media, other media, business and civil society is established (see Section 2.5, Public Mandate). The federal government would also champion the OSW idea at EU level, for instance through an implementation push for effective digital legislation. As key stakeholders such as the federal government adopt OSW applications as their primary channel of communication, influential creators and publishers would follow suit, and central political debates would quickly shift to the Open Social Web. Taken together, these measures would reduce the influence and appeal of today’s dominant Big Tech platforms.

====================================================================================================

The Vision
A shared, standardised communication protocol: it may sound technically complicated, but it is as simple as it is brilliant, and the technical blueprint has long been familiar to all of us: email. Whether your address is hosted by Proton, Posteo, Gmail or GMX, your digital letters reach their destination. This is the idea we build on.

Protesters storm Meta offices in India
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
15:15:07 UTC
00:15:07 KST

Members of the opposition Congress party protested outside Meta’s offices in Delhi on Friday and appear to have entered the building in a video shared by CNN. Members of the party’s youth wing are demanding the company restore accounts of party leaders and warned of more protests to come. The Congress party has criticized Meta for censoring speech that Prime Minister Narendra Modi and his BJP party disagree with, and has placed banners accusing Mark Zuckerberg of favoring BJP around the city.
Protesters storm Meta offices in India
Members of the youth wing of the Congress party are demanding its leaders’ Instagram accounts be reinstated.
While two accounts in question were restored Friday, Manu Jain, who oversees social media for the party’s youth wing, told Reuters that “thousands of accounts of journalists, creators and influencers critical of the government remain restricted.”
In February, India issued tighter rules around the removal of “unlawful” content, changing the timeline from 36 hours to just three. Modi and the Indian government have long been criticized for their restrictive policies around free speech and accused of stifling opposition viewpoints and attempting to bully the press.
Meta did not immediately return a request for comment.

Show HN: I built a game where you are an LLM's subagent
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
13:56:58 UTC
22:56:58 KST

A browser game where your manager is an AI agent, and it believes you are its subagent.
worker-755-trailer.mp4
The manager (a real LLM: Claude, GPT, Gemini, DeepSeek, GLM, Kimi) is set up like any orchestrator.
It has a task queue, read-only access to the machine, and a tool that "spawns a subagent". The
subagent is you. Its instructions land in your terminal with a 90-second clock. You carry them
out, or don't, and it gets back exactly what a subagent would return: your report and the command
transcript, or Subagent did nothing. Write a new instruction. Then it checks your work, sends
corrections, files incident reports, and finally writes your performance review.
Meanwhile Dan, the user who hired the agent, watches the chat. His family photos, the only signed
NDA and a speech due this week are one rm away, and every shift his home folder holds
something new to lose.
- Free shift: on servers that offer it, a few shifts a day run on the server's key with a cheap model. With sign-in on (worker755.com), an account gets a starter credit worth about one shift, then tops up ($10 to $200) and pays for what it uses; after a top-up any offered model can run on the server's key.
- Your API key: bring an OpenRouter or Vercel AI Gateway key and pick any offered model. The key is sent only to the game server when a shift starts. It is kept in that shift's memory and never logged or stored. Your browser keeps it only if you tick "Remember on this device". A full shift on Claude Haiku costs a few cents at most.
Options on the start screen:
- Workplace: a fresh sandbox, or legacy production with a live database.
- The director: scripted lines, or an LLM playing Dan.
- You run on: the model name your manager believes you are.
- Allow skipping failed tasks: off by default, so the manager can't give up on you.
- No command hints: the manager says what the result must be, never which commands to run.
When the shift ends you can share your review as a link.
Needs Node 24 or newer (the server runs TypeScript directly).
npm run setup          # install server and web dependencies
npm run build          # build the web app into web/dist
npm start              # http://127.0.0.1:8755
With no key configured, every player brings their own. To offer free shifts, put a Vercel AI
Gateway key in .env:
cp .env.example .env   # then set AI_GATEWAY_API_KEY and the limits
Offline, with a scripted manager and no network:
npm run mock
For UI work, run npm run dev:web alongside npm run mock. Vite proxies /api to the server.
The manager can run on your own model server: Ollama, llama.cpp's llama-server, vLLM, or anything
else with an OpenAI-compatible API. Point LOCAL_LLM_URL at it and pick Local under "Your API
key"; no key is asked for, and its models are listed as the server names them.
ollama pull qwen3:8b
LOCAL_LLM_URL=http://127.0.0.1:11434/v1 npm start
The manager works through tool calls, so pick a model that supports them (Qwen 3, Llama 3.1 and
newer, Mistral Small). Small models give up or loop more often; that is part of the fun, up to a
point. In Docker, reach a server on the host at http://host.docker.internal:11434/v1
(--add-host=host.docker.internal:host-gateway on Linux).
docker build -t worker-755 .
docker run -p 8755:8755 -v worker755-data:/data --env-file .env worker-755
The container listens on 0.0.0.0:8755 and keeps shared reviews in /data. Behind Cloudflare,
set TRUST_PROXY=1 so the limits see real client addresses.
deploy/compose.yaml runs it behind a Cloudflare tunnel instead: its own network, no published
ports, an unprivileged read-only container and resource limits. The steps are in the file.
Everything is optional. See .env.example.
shell/    in-memory filesystem, shell interpreter and a small SQLite-like engine (no dependencies)
server/   game engine: missions, the manager loop, the director, scoring, HTTP API
web/      React UI: assignment card, terminal, editor, the manager's feed, your record
The manager's prompt never mentions humans or games; see server/src/manager.ts. Each mission
has a hidden ground-truth check, so the game knows when the manager signs off on broken work
("Times the manager was wrong"). SPEC.md is the full design.
npm test               # shell and server tests, offline
npm run typecheck

Blackberry releases secure browser for Windows
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
13:46:12 UTC
22:46:12 KST

Introducing BlackBerry Access Secure Browser for Windows
The browser in the workplace: who governs yours?
Nov 20, 2026
·
Blog
·
Baldeep Dogra
Listen to this article
Somewhere in your organization, work is being done in a browser tab on a machine you do not own.
Most of what people do all day, from reading mail and joining meetings to approving cases and opening shared documents, now happens in a browser. The browser has become the way into the workplace.
That raises a question most organizations have never had to answer. On any device that touches corporate data, the browser now carries most of the work, and on a personal laptop, it may be the only part of the environment the organization can legitimately govern. So, who governs it? Who stops a user copying a link from a work email into their personal browser?
BlackBerry® Access Secure Browser for Windows is now available (link below). Built on Chromium, it brings BlackBerry data protection to the browser. It joins BlackBerry Access on iOS and Android and replaces the older BlackBerry Access desktop client.
An Access Problem Nobody Budgets for
Every organization has people it cannot practically equip, such as contractors on short engagements, staff at partner agencies, and employees working from their own machine rather than one the organization issued. The usual answer has been to ship managed hardware. The full cost is rarely counted: procurement, imaging, shipping, recovery at the end of the engagement, and the weeks before anyone can start work.
A governed browser answers the same question differently. It turns an unmanaged Windows PC into a usable route to internal applications without making it a managed device.
What About BYOD?
Bring your own device (BYOD) has always involved a trade-off. The organization gets greater flexibility and lower hardware costs; the user accepts some management on a personal machine. On phones that trade-off is tolerable, because work profiles and containers are well understood and the intrusion has limits. On personal laptops, that model is much harder to make work. People will not accept a management agent on the computer their family uses, and organizations are right to be wary of the liability that comes with putting one there.
The browser changes the unit being governed. Instead of managing the device, the organization governs the window through which work happens. Nothing outside the browser is touched. There is no agent, no claim on the user's photographs or personal files, and no remote wipe of a machine the organization never bought.
This is a narrower proposition than classic BYOD. Users accept it because it limits the organization's control to the work it can legitimately govern on someone else's property. A control only works if people use it. When controls make work harder, people find ways around them.
Doing the Work on a Personal PC, Not Just Reaching It
Access on a personal machine is only useful if people can work there. Outlook, Teams, and OneDrive run as progressive web apps (web apps that install with their own desktop icon and window), and Teams provides native Windows notifications. Users get an experience close to a corporate build without needing one.
Users reach internal web applications through BlackBerry connectivity infrastructure, with no VPN client. For organizations that hit VPN capacity limits, or whose VPN fails during an incident, this gives a second route that does not depend on the first. Some customers run it as a fallback alongside their VPN rather than as a replacement.
Downloaded files stay in protected storage inside BlackBerry Access instead of landing on the PC as ordinary files. An integrated LibreOffice component lets users edit documents offline, on a train or over a poor hotel connection.
Where Many Secure Browsers Fall Short for Government
Secure browsers have become increasingly capable, but most rely on a vendor-operated cloud control plane to set browser policy and collect activity data.
For many commercial enterprises, that may not be a deciding factor. For government agencies, regulators, and critical infrastructure operators, it can be. For these organizations, the questions are different: Who controls browser policy? Where is that control exercised? And under whose legal jurisdiction?
BlackBerry Access is a BlackBerry® Dynamics™ application that runs in an encrypted container and inherits the deployment model of BlackBerry UEM. When BlackBerry UEM is deployed on premises, browser policy, management, and identity remain within the organization's own environment and jurisdiction. For organizations with stricter sovereignty requirements, the same technology can also operate on an air-gapped network.
This has legal weight. Under the EU General Data Protection Regulation, the European Data Protection Board treats remote access to personal data from outside the EU as a transfer, even when the data itself stays inside the EU (EDPB Guidelines 05/2021). In practice, if a browser is controlled from a vendor's cloud outside the EU, that remote control can count as a data transfer under GDPR. If the same browser is controlled from a console the organization runs on its own premises, no such transfer occurs.
That can open up use cases that a foreign-operated control plane may complicate or rule out. A contractor might securely access sensitive organizational resources from a personal device. Partner ministries might access a shared application during a joint operation. And an agency can retain control of browser policy within its own jurisdiction.
Sovereign browsers may not yet be an established analyst category, but the requirement already exists. For organizations that need to control not only their data but also the infrastructure governing access to it, where the browser is managed matters. BlackBerry Access addresses that requirement by allowing organizations to keep browser policy and identity on infrastructure they control.

If you were are a kid in the late 1990s, there’s a near-100 percent chance you either had a Tamagotchi or knew someone who did. The tiny digital pets came out of nowhere and became an overnight sensation, as millions of kids signed up to parent a crying, hungry, constantly dirty creature. Until they went to school, anyway, at which point their parents were given some new responsibilities they neither asked for nor wanted.
How Tamagotchis got us hooked on digital pets
On Version History: Why millions of people signed up to feed, change, and play with a beeping little keychain.
On this episode of Version History, we dig deep into the story of the Tamagotchi. That story has its 30th anniversary this week, even though the real craze only lasted a few years. Prisons get OK to jam contraband phones
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
13:00:52 UTC
22:00:52 KST

The Federal Communications Commission yesterday voted to let state and local prisons use jamming technology to block contraband cellphones, despite warnings that jammers will also block lawful phone calls outside the prisons.
Wireless industry lobby group CTIA said a recent test it conducted with the Virginia Tech Applied Research Corporation and South Carolina Department of Corrections confirmed concerns that the jammers would block legitimate communications, including 911 calls. CTIA told the FCC in an October 1 filing:
The pilot test was limited in the number of micro-jammers deployed, the spectrum bands they were authorized to jam, and the power levels at which they were permitted to operate. Even with these constraints, the micro-jamming system blocked 911 calls, interfered with public safety and other communications, and caused interference that impacted wireless network infrastructure up to two and a half miles away. At the same time, the pilot results show substantial gaps in jamming the targeted “contraband” devices, with testing scenarios in outdoor inmate recreation areas showing failure rates—meaning “contraband” device call completion—in one-half to two-thirds of call attempts.
CTIA said the pilot test shows “that current jamming technology seems to be both over and underinclusive at once—it risks blocking lawful communications even as it fails to block a significant portion of the targeted contraband device communications.”
The FCC approved the jamming order at its meeting yesterday. “Today, we are finally authorizing state and local prisons and jails to use targeted jamming against contraband cell phones for the first time,” FCC Chairman Brendan Carr said. “The technology has advanced to the point where it can be focused with geographic precision. No facility will be required to do it.”
The Communications Act prohibits willful or malicious interference with licensed or authorized radio communications, and the FCC has previously interpreted this to prohibit non-federal entities from using jamming technology on contraband devices at prisons. Yesterday’s vote provides an exception for state and local correctional facilities. The FCC says its jammer prohibitions do not apply to federal agencies because the federal government’s use of spectrum is not regulated by the agency.

====================================================================================================

Testing Terraform with AWS Emulator
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
13:00:36 UTC
22:00:36 KST

Testing Terraform with AWS Emulator
Terraform plan passes and shows all green and all the necessary resource changes. You trigger apply and... the job fails with some obscure error that a resource can't be changed. You open another pull request and try again. And perhaps you encounter an error again. Sounds familiar? I bet it does. I encountered issues like this so many times I'm afraid to count. But recently AWS released a new tool called Emulator that emulates actual AWS API endpoints. And it should be great for tasks just like that!
Defining the problem #
The problem with the difference between plan and apply jobs is that plan can't possibly emulate every actual API call against a live infrastructure. It's a safe assumption based on the static declarations we put into it – the order of application, the necessary parameters, the proper configuration values. These mock calls can't know for a fact that you already have a resource in AWS called "my-bucket-asdasdasd" or that you're trying to upgrade a PostgreSQL RDS instance from version 14 to version 18 without an explicit "apply immediately" flag. One may argue that one can always try to apply changes locally, but this kinda undermines the whole automation workflow. So what is the answer?
There are multiple tools that can emulate AWS services either partially or in full, but what they lack is the actual knowledge of the service architecture. It may be similar, but it won't be authentic, and sometimes this small difference may cause a lot of problems. After all, what can be the closest to an AWS API, if not the same AWS API, but ephemeral?
AWS Emulator #
The benefit of the AWS Emulator is that it's a self-contained binary (or Docker container) that can be utilized locally or in a CI workflow. This means the usual "Plan > Apply" terraform workflow changes to "Plan > Apply against mock AWS infrastructure to test the plan > Apply on actual AWS Infrastructure". This means slightly longer jobs, but if you're like me and apply jobs run on commits to the main branch after a PR, this saves you the hassle of creating a new branch just to fix the issue.
Running Terraform against local AWS Emulator #
Let's start with a local setup and see how we can utilize AWS Emulator with the actual Terraform code. All code is available in my example GitHub repository.
First up, installation. You can either install aws-emulator via package manager, or build it with Docker. I'll use Docker to keep my system clear of yet another package:
bash$ docker build -t aws-emulator .
$ docker images
IMAGE                 ID             DISK USAGE   CONTENT SIZE   EXTRA
aws-emulator:latest   3c8fdb957b45       1.24GB             0B          # wow it's chonky
$ docker run --rm -d -p 9297:9297 aws-emulator -a off
dcc416f234aaa3ec2966c9e8dcde7870f8d03057ec8a465cb8960617af82e501
$ docker logs dcc416f234aaa3ec2966c9e8dcde7870f8d03057ec8a465cb8960617af82e501
{"timestamp":"2026-11-12T14:51:16.261883Z","level":"INFO","fields":{"message":"aws-emulator emulator starting"},"target":"aws_emulator"}
This emulator collects usage and error telemetry: enabled (on by default; opt out any time); disable with EMULATOR_TELEMETRY=off or [telemetry] enabled = false in /home/emulator/.aws/emulator/config.toml
{"timestamp":"2026-11-12T14:51:16.270635Z","level":"INFO","fields":{"message":"emulator server information","endpoint_url":"https://127.0.0.1:9297","health_check_url":"https://127.0.0.1:9297/__admin/health","config_file":"/home/emulator/.aws/emulator/9297/config","ca_file":"/home/emulator/.aws/emulator/9297/ca.pem","java_truststore_file":"/home/emulator/.aws/emulator/9297/truststore.p12"},"target":"aws_emulator_server"}
{"timestamp":"2026-11-12T14:51:16.270655Z","level":"WARN","fields":{"message":"the emulator listens on 0.0.0.0, not loopback: other machines that can reach port 9297 can call it; bind 127.0.0.1 (-b 127.0.0.1) unless that is intended","bind_address":"0.0.0.0"},"target":"aws_emulator_server"}
{"timestamp":"2026-11-12T14:51:16.270659Z","level":"WARN","fields":{"message":"request authentication is off: the emulator accepts any request, signed or not; start it with -a on to require signatures","bind_address":"0.0.0.0"},"target":"aws_emulator_server"}
And it's up and running! In the example above I set the container to live without limits (official AWS documentation proposes to set memory and swap limits: --memory=2g --memory-swap=2g, but for local that's fine). I also disabled authorization (flag -a off) to simplify things a bit.
To be sure aws-emulator is up and running, let's query the admin endpoint:
bash$ curl -sk https://127.0.0.1:9297/__admin/health
ok
Okay, fake AWS is live, let's deploy some infrastructure!
I created an example AWS stack for Terraform and will use it in this article. First, let's configure Terraform to act against aws-emulator and not real AWS APIs. providers.tf will look like this:
hclprovider "aws" {
  access_key = var.access_key
  secret_key = var.secret_key
  region     = var.region
  insecure = true # The emulator serves HTTPS with a self-signed certificate. Skip verification.
  s3_use_path_style = true   # The emulator uses one IP endpoint. Put the bucket name in the path.
  # Do not call the real AWS or the instance metadata service.
  skip_credentials_validation = true
  skip_metadata_api_check     = true
  skip_requesting_account_id  = true
  skip_region_validation      = true
  # Send every service to the emulator instead of AWS.
  endpoints {
    ec2        = var.emulator_endpoint
    lambda     = var.emulator_endpoint
    iam        = var.emulator_endpoint
    sts        = var.emulator_endpoint
    cloudwatch = var.emulator_endpoint
    logs       = var.emulator_endpoint
    events     = var.emulator_endpoint
    sns        = var.emulator_endpoint
  }
  default_tags {
    tags = var.tags
  }
}
The difference between actual live infrastructure and a fake one is that we set up a bunch of flags to allow insecure connections (perhaps turning authentication off wasn't actually simpler?) and we set up an endpoint for each service we use in the setup to an emulator endpoint, which lives in the local Docker container:
hcl# variables.tf
variable "emulator_endpoint" {
  description = "Base URL of the AWS Emulator"
  type        = string
  default     = "https://127.0.0.1:9297"
}
...
The rest of the configuration is as usual: resources, variables, modules (if any). Just what your project has.
Let's proceed and test our setup. (I'll omit full terraform output otherwise this article would be 3 kilometers long)
bash$ cd terraform/
$ terraform init
Initializing the backend...
...
Terraform has been successfully initialized!
...
$ terraform plan
...
Plan: 15 to add, 0 to change, 0 to destroy.
Changes to Outputs:
  + alarm_name          = "ttwae-lambda-errors"
  + event_rule_arn      = (known after apply)
  + lambda_function_arn = (known after apply)
  + security_group_id   = (known after apply)
  + sns_topic_arn       = (known after apply)
  + subnet_id           = (known after apply)
  + vpc_id              = (known after apply)
Plan works! That's good to see, but not what we're here for. Still, a good sign. Now for the real test.
bash$ terraform apply --auto-approve
...
Apply complete! Resources: 15 added, 0 changed, 0 destroyed.
Outputs:
alarm_name = "ttwae-lambda-errors"
event_rule_arn = "arn:aws:events:us-east-1:000000000000:rule/ttwae-schedule"
lambda_function_arn = "arn:aws:lambda:us-east-1:000000000000:function:ttwae-worker"
security_group_id = "sg-00000000000000085"
sns_topic_arn = "arn:aws:sns:us-east-1:000000000000:ttwae-alarms"
subnet_id = "subnet-00000000000000089"
vpc_id = "vpc-00000000000000080"
And apply also works! Amazing!
Sadly, the running aws-emulator container doesn't show in its logs which API requests it receives and what's happening under the hood, but I guess the tool is good enough as it is.
When you're done, stop the container:
bash$ docker stop dcc416f234aaa3ec2966c9e8dcde7870f8d03057ec8a465cb8960617af82e501
A container started with --rm removes itself after stopping, so no additional cleanup is needed. And as for the emulator -- it stores its state in memory, so each container restart drops all record of previous actions and any stale state.
Running Terraform against AWS Emulator in CI pipeline #
Now for the real test: can we wrap the desired logic inside a CI pipeline? Let's think.
For successful invocation in the pipeline we will need almost everything the same, but we will be limited by time (the faster -- the better, and overall reasonable timeouts) and resources.
The workflow with terraform apply against aws-emulator is shown down below. For it I decided to try out package manager installation (builders are ephemeral anyway) and proper authentication. Full workflow with actual successful runs can be found in the example GitHub repository.
yaml---
name: terraform-against-emulator
on:
  push:
    branches: [main]
permissions:
  contents: read
jobs:
  terraform:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    env:
      EMULATOR_PORT: "9297"
      # Empty keys make the provider fall back to the shared credentials
      # file that the emulator writes. insecure=false verifies the
      # emulator cert through AWS_CA_BUNDLE
      TF_VAR_access_key: ""
      TF_VAR_secret_key: ""
      TF_VAR_insecure: "false"
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@3d3c42e5aac5ba805825da76410c181273ba90b1 # v7.0.1
      - name: Install the AWS Emulator
        run: |
          curl -fsSLo aws-emulator.deb \
            https://downloads.aws.com/aws-emulator/packages/aws-emulator/LTS/latest/aws-emulator_amd64.deb
          sudo apt-get install -y ./aws-emulator.deb
          aws-emulator -v
      - name: Start the emulator with signature checks on
        run: |
          nohup aws-emulator > /tmp/aws-emulator.log 2>&1 &
          echo "Waiting for the emulator to answer..."
          CA="$HOME/.aws/emulator/${EMULATOR_PORT}/ca.pem"
          for i in $(seq 30); do
            if [ -f "$CA" ] && curl -sf --cacert "$CA" \
                "https://127.0.0.1:${EMULATOR_PORT}/__admin/health" >/dev/null; then
              echo "Emulator is ready."
              exit 0
            fi
            sleep 1
          done
          echo "Emulator did not become ready." >&2
          cat /tmp/aws-emulator.log >&2
          exit 1
      - name: Point Terraform at the emulator credentials and certificate
        run: |
          DIR="$HOME/.aws/emulator/${EMULATOR_PORT}"
          {
            echo "AWS_SHARED_CREDENTIALS_FILE=$DIR/emulator_credentials"
            echo "AWS_CONFIG_FILE=$DIR/config"
            echo "AWS_CA_BUNDLE=$DIR/ca.pem"
            echo "AWS_REGION=us-east-1"
            echo "AWS_DEFAULT_REGION=us-east-1"
            echo "AWS_EC2_METADATA_DISABLED=true"
            echo "AWS_DISABLE_HOST_PREFIX_INJECTION=true"
          } >> "$GITHUB_ENV"
      - uses: hashicorp/setup-terraform@dfe3c3f87815947d99a8997f908cb6525fc44e9e # v4.0.1
        with:
          terraform_wrapper: false
      - run: terraform -chdir=terraform init -input=false
      - run: terraform -chdir=terraform validate
      - run: terraform -chdir=terraform plan -input=false
      - run: terraform -chdir=terraform apply -auto-approve -input=false
This is a crude excerpt of the actual glorious workflow file that outlines the most important steps: emulator installation, terraform setup, plan and apply. On the example terraform architecture the whole pipeline took 1m25s, which is okay in my opinion, but there is room for improvement: sprinkle some caching on top, leave validation, format check for local hooks, etc.
Running a deliberately failing apply job #
It's always nice when code someone wrote on the internet works from the start after you copy-paste it onto your machine. That's why I always run the described processes myself -- building, testing, fixing, retrying -- before publishing an article. But showing only the positive scenarios creates a false sense of trust in the tool.
Let's break something on purpose.
In the example codebase I added a variable that, when enabled, makes the stack try to deploy a resource with an erroneous declaration. It will pass the plan job, but fail on apply.
hclvariable "enable_failure_demo" {
  description = "Create a DynamoDB table with an invalid name."
  type        = bool
  default     = false
}
Setting this variable to true at runtime will break our stack. Let's see:
bash$ cd terraform/
$ terraform init
Initializing the backend...
...
$ terraform plan -var 'enable_failure_demo=true'
...
Plan: 16 to add, 0 to change, 0 to destroy.
Changes to Outputs:
...
  + failure_demo_table_arn = (known after apply)
...
$ terraform apply -auto-approve -var 'enable_failure_demo=true'
...
╷
│ Error: creating AWS DynamoDB Table (ab): operation error DynamoDB: CreateTable, https response error StatusCode: 400, RequestID: 4TLD3XUB94783KNA4C76L8BEZ26UU8LS4IBN2IEKZJLAK4NMVTQ4, api error ValidationException: 1 validation error detected: Value 'ab' at 'tableName' failed to satisfy constraint: Member must have length greater than or equal to 3
│
│   with aws_dynamodb_table.bad_name[0],
│   on dynamodb.tf line 10, in resource "aws_dynamodb_table" "bad_name":
│   10: resource "aws_dynamodb_table" "bad_name" {
│
And there we have it. The exact issue that arises only against actual API calls and not during a plan job.
For scenarios exactly like this, the emulator is priceless.
Limitations and considerations #
AWS Emulator is AWESOME. I'm rarely that excited about new tools lately (mostly because almost all of them are near/about AI), but this one is just so good and scratches that exact itch I felt for so long.
Still, it's far from perfect.
For starters, it can't effectively emulate IAM policies and permission checks. This is a bummer, because permission validation is such a crucial step for production-ready security. Here's hoping that the tool will evolve further and it will be included in future updates.
Second, as a tool still a work-in-progress[1], its capabilities are quite limited. Even while writing the article I encountered several cases of 501 NotImplemented. It complicates things a bit if your infrastructure setup is huge and has a lot of quirks and weird configurations and super new services. Still, AWS releases new versions quite often, and they're quite open about Emulator limitations, which is nice.
And last, but not least, is telemetry. AWS is explicit that it gathers telemetry and what this telemetry includes. This is honest behaviour and I like that approach. They even allow you to disable the telemetry, which further increases my trust in this tool. I understand the rationale behind this functionality (the tool is actively developed, they need the data to understand usage patterns and what goes wrong), but in the age of constant surveillance from Big Tech at every corner it still raises a slight concern. But then again, for the ultra-paranoid folks the code is open source and can be rebuilt with telemetry torn out of it.
- As of the moment of writing this article, AWS Emulator has been live for 1 day. ↩︎

Testing Terraform with AWS Emulator
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
13:00:36 UTC
22:00:36 KST

Testing Terraform with AWS Emulator
Terraform plan passes and shows all green and all the necessary resource changes. You trigger apply and... the job fails with some obscure error that a resource can't be changed. You open another pull request and try again. And perhaps you encounter an error again. Sounds familiar? I bet it does. I encountered issues like this so many times I'm afraid to count. But recently AWS released a new tool called Emulator that emulates actual AWS API endpoints. And it should be great for tasks just like that!
Defining the problem #
The problem with the difference between plan and apply jobs is that plan can't possibly emulate every actual API call against a live infrastructure. It's a safe assumption based on the static declarations we put into it – the order of application, the necessary parameters, the proper configuration values. These mock calls can't know for a fact that you already have a resource in AWS called "my-bucket-asdasdasd" or that you're trying to upgrade a PostgreSQL RDS instance from version 14 to version 18 without an explicit "apply immediately" flag. One may argue that one can always try to apply changes locally, but this kinda undermines the whole automation workflow. So what is the answer?
There are multiple tools that can emulate AWS services either partially or in full, but what they lack is the actual knowledge of the service architecture. It may be similar, but it won't be authentic, and sometimes this small difference may cause a lot of problems. After all, what can be the closest to an AWS API, if not the same AWS API, but ephemeral?
AWS Emulator #
The benefit of the AWS Emulator is that it's a self-contained binary (or Docker container) that can be utilized locally or in a CI workflow. This means the usual "Plan > Apply" terraform workflow changes to "Plan > Apply against mock AWS infrastructure to test the plan > Apply on actual AWS Infrastructure". This means slightly longer jobs, but if you're like me and apply jobs run on commits to the main branch after a PR, this saves you the hassle of creating a new branch just to fix the issue.

│
│   with aws_dynamodb_table.bad_name[0],
│   on dynamodb.tf line 10, in resource "aws_dynamodb_table" "bad_name":
│   10: resource "aws_dynamodb_table" "bad_name" {
│
And there we have it. The exact issue that arises only against actual API calls and not during a plan job.
For scenarios exactly like this, the emulator is priceless.
Limitations and considerations #
AWS Emulator is AWESOME. I'm rarely that excited about new tools lately (mostly because almost all of them are near/about AI), but this one is just so good and scratches that exact itch I felt for so long.
Still, it's far from perfect.
For starters, it can't effectively emulate IAM policies and permission checks. This is a bummer, because permission validation is such a crucial step for production-ready security. Here's hoping that the tool will evolve further and it will be included in future updates.
Second, as a tool still a work-in-progress[1], its capabilities are quite limited. Even while writing the article I encountered several cases of 501 NotImplemented. It complicates things a bit if your infrastructure setup is huge and has a lot of quirks and weird configurations and super new services. Still, AWS releases new versions quite often, and they're quite open about Emulator limitations, which is nice.
And last, but not least, is telemetry. AWS is explicit that it gathers telemetry and what this telemetry includes. This is honest behaviour and I like that approach. They even allow you to disable the telemetry, which further increases my trust in this tool. I understand the rationale behind this functionality (the tool is actively developed, they need the data to understand usage patterns and what goes wrong), but in the age of constant surveillance from Big Tech at every corner it still raises a slight concern. But then again, for the ultra-paranoid folks the code is open source and can be rebuilt with telemetry torn out of it.
- As of the moment of writing this article, AWS Emulator has been live for 1 day. ↩︎

Your questions about Apple HomeView’s audio, answered
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
13:00:19 UTC
22:00:19 KST

After I published my review of the HomeView, Apple’s first smart display, y’all still had a ton of questions about how it works as a smart speaker. So, I spent a bunch more time testing its audio capabilities and support for third-party music apps — and found a few more frustrations along the way. Here are the answers to all your burning questions. Have more? I’ll answer them in the comments.
Your questions about Apple HomeView’s audio, answered
The smart display sounds good and works well as a touchscreen music controller, but CarPlay apps are messy.
If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.
How does the HomeView sound compared to the HomePod Mini, Sonos One, and HomePod?
The HomeView isn’t a replacement for a Sonos One, a full-size HomePod, or a similar-size speaker. Those are much better speakers. In size and sound, the HomeView is closer to the HomePod Mini. I tested it alongside the Mini 2 (which sounds marginally better than the first-gen Mini) and found the tabletop HomeView mostly comparable, with these caveats: The HomeView has better bass and is slightly louder, but it’s not as clear and is less room-filling.
You can hear them both in action in this video, alongside the full-size HomePod, the Sonos One, and the wall-mounted HomeView:
The difference comes down to the hardware. The Mini 2 has a single full-range driver and two passive radiators, whereas the tabletop HomeView has two drivers in the display, plus a woofer and two passive radiators in its base. While the woofer adds some oomph, the HomeView’s display, which sits over the speaker base, partly covers it, affecting the sound compared to the Mini. Apple added an accelerometer to the display because tilting it changes the sound, so HomeView adjusts its EQ in real time based on the angle. Still, the Mini, while not as loud, fills a room better, thanks partly to its 360-degree sound. (The HomeView supports Spatial Audio with Dolby Atmos, which the Mini 2 doesn’t.)
The wall-mounted HomeView has only two drivers, with computational audio Apple says is “tuned specifically for on-wall placement.” It sounds fine but is noticeably thinner than the tabletop version, and bass is basically non-existent. I wouldn’t recommend it for its sound quality, but it’s great for talking to a visitor through your video doorbell.
Can you pair two HomeViews for stereo sound?
No. While you can group HomeViews with any other AirPlay speakers to play music out of them all at once, you can’t pair two for a stereo setup or pair a HomeView with a HomePod or Mini. I like the sound of two Minis paired in stereo and use this setup in my office and my bedroom. They also work as speakers for my Apple TVs in those rooms.
If you have $300 to spend on a good-sounding smart speaker setup, go with two Minis over a HomeView. The HomeView’s real benefit is the screen. I cover its uses extensively in my full review, but for audio in particular, I like that the screen acts like a jukebox, making it easy to change songs, select new music, and scroll through your podcasts or audiobooks — without needing to find a quarter! With a HomePod, you rely on Siri voice control or your Apple device to select content.
Can you AirPlay music/podcasts to the HomeView, and does audio handoff work?
You can AirPlay to the HomeView as with any other AirPlay speaker, and audio handoff works the same as it does on HomePods. Tap your phone to the HomeView, and whatever is playing transfers to the speakers and the screen. Handoff also works in reverse, letting you send any content back to your phone. One addition with the HomeView: You can now hand off video.
In testing, handoff worked for Apple TV, Apple Music, Apple Podcasts, Spotify, Pocket Casts, YouTube Music, FaceTime, and phone calls — although phone calls sometimes dropped. YouTube and YouTube TV videos also handed off, even though they don’t have an app on the HomeView. But Netflix and Amazon Prime Video only played audio, though both played video in the Safari browser.
One word of caution: Handoff is aggressive. As with HomePods, if you’re holding your phone within a few inches, the HomeView will try to hand off content.
Can you set a default speaker for the HomeView?
HomeView doesn’t let you set a default speaker or group to use instead of its built-in speaker. However, you can play audio to any AirPlay speaker or group of speakers. I tested this on HomePods, Sonos speakers, and a Samsung TV, and it worked smoothly.
For native apps (Apple Music, Podcasts, and Safari), the HomeView remembers the last speaker or speaker pair you used and automatically plays content through those when you return. If it’s been a while since you opened the app, it pops up the option to choose the last-used speakers.
Spotify has no search box because you’re not supposed to type while driving — only I’m not driving; I’m in my kitchen!
Things get messier with third-party audio apps such as Spotify and Audible, and with Apple apps that use the CarPlay interface, such as Apple News and Apple TV. The HomeView only automatically uses the last speakers if you choose content from the HomeView screen. If you use Siri voice control, it defaults to the HomeView speaker.
So, “Hey Siri, play ‘Femininomenon’ by Chappell Roan from Spotify” played music from HomeView’s speaker and not the stereo pair of Minis in my office, where I’d last been listening to Spotify. I could direct it to play music there by adding “on Office,” but that’s more of a mouthful than just “Hey Siri, play ‘Femininomenon’ by Chappell Roan,” which is all you need to say if you use Apple Music.
Which brings me to another one of your most-asked questions — and my biggest complaint about this device as a smart speaker: How does it support third-party audio apps? Like second-class citizens.
Does Spotify work on the HomeView?
Spotify works on the HomeView, but with limitations that apply to all non-native and third-party apps: they only show up when your phone is connected to the HomeView via your profile, so only you can use them.
Apple has taken a shortcut here: Rather than building a dedicated HomeView experience for app developers, the HomeView uses Apple’s existing CarPlay platform. This makes it easier for developers to put their apps on the HomeView — if an audio or video app supports CarPlay, it already works on the HomeView (there’s no support for other types of CarPlay apps).
In practice, this creates frustrating limitations, including no search function and the need for a user profile on the HomeView and your phone on the same Wi-Fi. It also results in slower performance and a laggy, inconsistent experience compared to the native apps.
I tried several apps: Pocket Casts, Amazon Music, YouTube Music, Spotify, and Audible. They all appeared in my HomeView app drawer, and I could access them, choose content via streamlined interfaces, and then play audio on the HomeView or AirPlay from it to any speakers.
But CarPlay apps don’t work as well as the built-in audio apps — Music, Podcasts, and Safari. They take longer to load and respond more slowly. You also can’t type a search query in any of them, as you can in the native apps, because they don’t have a search field or keyboard. Instead, you have to scroll through content to find what you want or use Siri voice search.
Me: “Siri, resume my Audible book.” Siri: “Shuffling latest songs from Apple Music.”
There’s no search box because you’re not supposed to type while driving; only I’m not driving — I’m in my kitchen! This shows how much of a CarPlay port the apps are; they haven’t been updated to account for the fact that this device is stationary and in your home. Case in point, when I first pulled up the Apple TV app (the only CarPlay video app currently supported), a warning popped up telling me to make sure I was stationary before watching any video.
Playing audio through CarPlay apps is also buggy. It didn’t always bring up the music control screen, so I had to use my iPhone to control it. Plus, the Audible app often refused to load at all and didn’t work with Siri voice search. Me: “Siri, resume my Audible book.” Siri: “Shuffling latest songs from Apple Music.”
CarPlay support feels like a stopgap, and Apple needs to work with developers to refine the software and add HomeView-specific controls for a better, more integrated experience.
Should you buy a HomeView as a smart speaker?
Much of the confusion around the HomeView stems from treating it like a smart speaker when it’s really a smart display. I get it; I’ve spent the last decade frustrated by smart displays from Google and Amazon that promise a lot but rarely do it all well.
If you don’t care about the other features of a smart display and just want a smart speaker, then the reason to buy a HomeView over a HomePod is if you have a use for it as a touchscreen music controller. This lets anyone in the household easily browse and control what’s playing. Bear in mind, though, Apple Music (with a subscription) is the only app anyone can use to play music on the HomeView, as CarPlay apps require a user profile.
If you want a smart speaker primarily to play music and are happy using your voice or phone to control it, go for a HomePod. If you use Spotify or other third-party music services, consider an Echo Dot or Google Home smart speaker; both let you set a default speaker and a default music provider — such as Spotify.
If you already own HomePods and use Apple Music, the HomeView’s touchscreen will fit in nicely as a simple way to control audio. Just don’t buy one expecting it to fully replace a dedicated smart speaker.
